\end{figure}

Section~\ref{subsec:selecting_active_dofs} selects the active DoFs of level $\l$ by the subtraction in Equation~\eqref{eq:active_k_form_dofs}: the open DoFs of the extended region $\extendedPatch{\M}_\l$, minus the open DoFs of the ancestor patch $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$, which the next level re-supplies at half the mesh size. This exchange of DoFs being clean in the right sense is what guarantees linear independence and the correct cohomology for the adaptive subdivision $k$-form spaces, see~\cite{companion_paper}. It is clean as long as the refined and extended region $\guidance{\extendedPatch{\M}_{\l+1}}$ \guidance{does not merge patches that are disconnected in} the ancestor region $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$. Looking at Figure~\ref{fig:region_anatomy}, we can see that this merging could happen when patches of $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$ would meet in one vertex or have only one strip of coarse faces between, since the extension would bridge that gap. The admissibility conditions presented in this section can be understood as enforcing a minimum distance between two patches to avoid these problematic geometries.

The geometric predicates of this section are local tests for the ways that distance can collapse. All of are read off a single object, the \emph{refinement fan} of a vertex, which collects the faces of the mesh at that vertex that the sequence refines beyond the current level.

\begin{definition}[Refinement fan]
    \label{def:refinement_fan}
    Let $\domseq[\L]$ be a domain sequence with regions $\M_\l$. The \emph{refinement fan} $\refFan{\l} \big( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \big)$ of a vertex $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V_\l(\T_\l)$ contains all incident faces of $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ that are refined beyond level $\l$ in the domain sequence, i.e.
    \begin{equation}
        \refFan{\l} \big( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \coloneq \FVl{\l} \big( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \cap \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}.
    \end{equation}
    \robert{The definition is read on every level $\lzero \leq \l \leq \L$, and at the finest one it is empty for every vertex, since Definition~\ref{def:domseq} sets $\M_{\L+1} = \varnothing$ and hence $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\L+1}} = \varnothing$. A domain sequence carries nothing beyond level $\L$, so no face of $\T_\L$ is refined beyond its own level.}
\end{definition}

An empty refinement fan implies that vertex at level $\l$ is outside of the region $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$ of $\domseq[\L]$, and thus exterior to any refinement beyond $\l$. The following definition formalises that idea.

\begin{definition}[Refinement-exterior vertex]
    \label{def:refinement_exterior_vertex}
    Let $\domseq[\L]$ be a domain sequence. A vertex $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V_\l(\T_\l)$ is \emph{refinement-exterior} at level $\l$ if its refinement fan in $\domseq[\L]$ is empty on that level, i.e. $\refFan{\l} \big( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \big) = \varnothing$.
\end{definition}

Note that a refinement-exterior vertex of $\domseq[\L]$ at a given level $\l$ need not be in the interior of $\T_\l$ but can also lie on its boundary $\partial \T_\l$. For example, Figures~\ref{fig:adm_exterior} and~\ref{fig:adm_faces} display domain sequences with refinement-exterior vertices at the domain boundary.

The refinement fan can only empty out. \guidance{If none of a vertex' incident faces lies in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$, then no face of $\M_{\l+1}$ contains it either, since each of those sits inside a coarse face that does not. The collar of the extension adds one strip of level-$(\l+1)$ faces, which is half a coarse face wide and therefore too narrow to bridge that gap, so the vertex stays outside $\extendedPatch{\M}_{\l+1}$ as well.} Thus, a vertex that is refinement-exterior at one level stays refinement-exterior at all finer ones. \guidance{Decision: the monotonicity is now free. The third case of Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} makes a vertex refinement-exterior exactly on the levels $\l \geq \lmax$, which is upward closed, so the first sentence of the note above can be replaced by a forward pointer. The collar sentence is a separate claim about $\extendedPatch{\M}_{\l+1}$ that the three cases do not cover, so it stays or gets settled on its own.} Every vertex therefore has a level at which it first becomes refinement-exterior, which is what Figure~\ref{fig:adm_exterior} colours it by. \robert{That level always exists and is at most $\L$, since the fan at the finest level is empty for every vertex, and Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} identifies it as the finest level $\lmax$ of the leaf star of the vertex, once the leaf star is available in Section~\ref{subsec:vertex_neighbourhoods}.} In this figure, the outermost layer is refinement-exterior already at level $\lzero$, because none of its incident faces is refined beyond that level, so its refinement fan is empty there according to Definition~\ref{def:refinement_fan}. The next layer becomes refinement-exterior only at level $1$, since at level $0$ it still has incident faces that are refined beyond that level. This logic applies to all layers in the figure until we reach the finest level $3$, at which the remaining vertices become refinement-exterior.

\smallskip

% OLD (pre-rename), delete once cherry-picked:
% The first geometric predicate concerns a leaf face enclosed by refinement. If none of its vertices is refinement-exterior, the finer level extension reaches the coarse face from every corner at once, which can lead to linear dependence or merging patches. The predicate names the harmless complement.
\guidance{The first geometric predicate concerns a leaf face that the refinement has closed in on. If none of its vertices is refinement-exterior, all of its corners lie in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$ and the finer level extension reaches the coarse face from every corner at once, which can lead to linear dependence or merging patches. The predicate calls such a face confined.}

\begin{definition}[Confined face]
    \label{def:confined_face}
    \robert{A leaf face $\face{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \leafF{\l}(\domseq[\L])$} is \emph{unconfined} if at least one of its vertices is refinement-exterior at level $\l$. Otherwise, it is \emph{confined}.
\end{definition}

The second problematic configuration is a vertex at which the refined part of its mesh star falls into two or more distinct patches meeting only at the vertex itself. The coarse faces that separate them are squeezed to a point there, so the extended refined patches are guaranteed to merge. \guidance{Counting these patches is what the next two definitions do.}

\begin{definition}[Refinement arcs of a vertex]
    \label{def:refinement_arcs}
    Let $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V(\domseq[\L])$. The \emph{refinement arcs of $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$} are the maximal runs of consecutive faces in its cyclic (or, at a boundary vertex, linear) refinement fan $\refFan{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$. \guidance{Two faces of the same arc are connected by a chain of faces of the fan, consecutive ones sharing a level-$\l$ edge at $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$, while faces of distinct arcs are not. The maximal runs of $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ that are \emph{not} in $\refFan{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ are the \emph{gaps} of $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$, cyclic at an interior vertex and linear at a boundary vertex.}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Pinch vertex]
    \label{def:pinch_vertex}
    A vertex $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V(\domseq[\L])$ is a \emph{pinch vertex} if it has two or more refinement arcs.
\end{definition}

At the domain boundary the same defect takes a second form that the number of pieces alone does not see. A single fine boundary vertex can be sealed off by coarse faces on both sides and touch the boundary only through the vertex. In that case, the open DoFs strip the single boundary vertex, while the open DoFs of the refined and extended version carry a boundary component.

\begin{definition}[Boundary pinch vertex]
    \label{def:boundary_pinch}
    Let $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V(\domseq[\L]) \cap \partial\T_\l$ lie on the boundary of $\T_\l$. \guidance{Exactly two faces of its mesh star $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ carry a domain boundary edge incident to $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$, namely the two ends of its linear fan. We call a refinement arc \emph{anchored} if it contains one of them.} The vertex $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ is a \emph{boundary pinch} if it has at least one refinement arc that is not anchored.
\end{definition}

\prose{We collect the simplices each predicate catches. Given a domain sequence $\domseq[\L]$ we write
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:confined_set}
    \F^{\mathrm{confined}}(\domseq[\L]) \; \coloneq \; \big\{ \face{\l}{\fineIdxone} \in \leafF{\l}(\domseq[\L]) \; \colon \; \face{\l}{\fineIdxone} \text{\; is confined} \big\}
\end{equation}
for the leaf faces caught by Definition~\ref{def:confined_face}, and
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pinch_set}
    \V^{\mathrm{pinch}}(\domseq[\L]) \; \coloneq \; \big\{ \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V(\domseq[\L]) \; \colon \;\; \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \;\text{ is a pinch vertex or a boundary pinch} \big\}
\end{equation}
for the vertices caught by Definitions~\ref{def:pinch_vertex} and~\ref{def:boundary_pinch}. A domain sequence is admissible in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:subdiv_partition} exactly when both sets are empty.}

    \begin{subfigure}[t]{\admScale\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{#1}
        \caption{#2}
        \label{#3}
    \end{subfigure}%
}
% -------------------------------------------------------------------------
\begin{figure}[tb]
    \centering
    \admsubfig
        {images/visualisations/v8_quarters_lvl1_pinch_marked.pdf}
        {Interior pinch with two refinement arcs}{fig:pinch_interior}
    \hfill
    \admsubfig
        {images/visualisations/v8_graded_top_bottom_sides_pinch_marked.pdf}
        {Non-pinch with a single refinement arc}{fig:pinch_single_arc}
    \hfill
    \admsubfig
        {images/visualisations/v6_boundary_topbottom_lvl1_pinch_marked.pdf}
        {Pinches at the domain boundary}{fig:pinch_boundary_two_arcs}
    \hfill
    \admsubfig
        {images/visualisations/v6_boundary_triangle_lvl1_pinch_marked.pdf}
        {Boundary pinches with unanchored arcs}{fig:pinch_boundary_unanchored}
    \caption{Four domain sequences (represented by their leaf mesh) with the potential pinch vertex marked in yellow and the faces of its refinement arcs shaded in purple, cf. Definitions~\ref{def:pinch_vertex} and~\ref{def:boundary_pinch}. Panels~\subref{fig:pinch_boundary_two_arcs} and~\subref{fig:pinch_boundary_unanchored} visualise one additional subtlety: boundary vertices can create pinches in two distinct ways, either by two refinement arcs as in~\subref{fig:pinch_boundary_two_arcs} or by one unanchored refinement arc as in~\subref{fig:pinch_boundary_unanchored}. The former case is considered an interior pinch that happens at the boundary, while the letter is considered a bondary pinch.}
    \label{fig:pinches}
\end{figure}

\smallskip

The predicates of face confinement and vertex pinches allow us to define \emph{admissible domain sequences} as a subset of domain sequences.

\begin{definition}[Admissible domain sequence]
    \label{def:subdiv_partition}
    A domain sequence $\domseq[\L](\T_\L)$ is an \emph{admissible domain sequence} iff
    \begin{itemize}
        \item[(i)] no leaf face of $\domseq[\L]$ is confined,
        \item[(ii)] no vertex of $\domseq[\L]$ is a pinch vertex,
        \item[(iii)] no domain boundary vertex of $\domseq[\L]$ is a boundary pinch vertex.
    \end{itemize}
    We denote the set of admissible domain sequences by $\allpartitions(\T_\L)$.
\end{definition}

Condition (ii) is deliberately not restricted to interior vertices to cover cases like the one in Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_boundary_two_arcs}. The three conditions are the three collapses ruled out one by one, \guidance{so together they keep distinct patches of $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$ far enough apart for the exchange of Section~\ref{subsec:selecting_active_dofs}, stated entirely in terms of one vertex or one face at a time, and~\cite{companion_paper} proves that they suffice.} 

The following two theorems are from ~\cite{companion_paper} and show that both linear independence and the correct cohomology of the adaptive subdivision $k$-form spaces follow from the admissibility of the domain sequence they are defined on.

\begin{theorem}[Linear independence, from~\cite{companion_paper}]
    \label{theorem:linear_independence}
    Let $\domseq \in \allpartitions$ be a admissible domain sequence of the mesh $\T_\L$. The active basis functions selected by Def.~\ref{def:active_k_form_dofs} of the adaptive spaces $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ are linearly independent.
\end{theorem}

The minimum gap between two patches enforced by the admissibility of the domain sequence also guarantees that the regions preserve their respective cohomology classes under refinement and extension, see~\cite{companion_paper} for a detailed derivation.

\begin{theorem}[Discrete de Rham complexes, from~\cite{companion_paper}]
    \label{theorem:exactness}
    Let $\domseq \in \allpartitions$ be a admissible domain sequence. Then, the cohomology groups of the complexes $\mathfrak{R}_{\domseq}$ and $\mathring{\mathfrak{R}}_{\domseq}$ of adaptive $k$-form subdivision spaces from Theorem~\ref{theorem:differential_complex} are isomorphic to the ones of absolute and relative de Rham complexes, respectively.
\end{theorem}

\FloatBarrier

\section{Refinement operators and their locality}
\label{sec:refinement_operators}

The previous section constrained the domain sequence and said which selections of active degrees of freedom are admissible. Everything in it is a property of the spaces on a fixed domain sequence. This and the next section construct the refinement operators we use to refine the domain sequence in the adaptive loop. This includes design choices to a certain extent, which we will address at the appropriate time. The definitions from here on are answerable to the algorithm rather than to the theorems, meaning that they are chosen so that the operators of Section~\ref{subsec:refinement_operators} can be stated and implemented, and a different refinement strategy may replace them while leaving Section~\ref{subsec:subdiv_partition} untouched.

\subsection{Vertex neighbourhoods for the refinement operators}
\label{subsec:vertex_neighbourhoods}

This subsection defines three neighbourhoods of a vertex, and they differ in what they are allowed to read. The \emph{mesh star} $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ of Section~\ref{subsec:selecting_active_dofs} collects the faces of $\T_\l$ incident to $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ and is a property of the uniform mesh alone, but it is only defined on the levels at which the vertex is a mesh vertex. The \emph{star ancestry} below removes that restriction while staying a property of the mesh hierarchy alone. The \emph{leaf star} defined afterwards is the only one of the three that reads the domain sequence.

\subsubsection{Faces and their ancestors across levels}
\label{subsubsec:faces_across_levels}

\begin{definition}[Face ancestor]
    \label{def:face_ancestor}
    Let $\face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone} \in \F_\lone(\T_\lone)$ be a face on level $\lone$ and choose a coarser level $\l$ such that $\lzero \leq \l \leq \lone$. The \emph{face ancestor} operator $\anc{\l}$ sends $\face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone}$ to its ancestor $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ on level $\l$ by the identity
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:face_ancestor}
        \anc{\l} \big( \face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone} \big) = \face{\l}{\fineIdxone} \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone} \in \Aop{}{\l}{\lone} \big( \face{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \big).
    \end{equation}
    The face ancestor operator extends to sets $\simplexsubsetTwo{\lone} \in \F_{\lone}(\T_\lone)$ of faces on level $\lone$ by
    \begin{equation}
        \anc{\l}(\simplexsubsetTwo{\lone}) = \bigcup_{\face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone} \in \simplexsubsetTwo{\lone}} \; \anc{\l} \big(\face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone} \big).
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}


Both the face ancestor and the patch ancestor only operator on mesh hierarchy, not on a domain sequence. The difference between them is that face ancestor operator only needs ``$\in$'' instead of the ``$=$'' in Definition~\ref{def:ancestor_patch} of the ancestor patch. This is why the patch ancestor carries the assumption of quadrisection-closedness, while the face ancestor does not require that.

Face Ancestry composes, i.e. $\anc{\l} \circ \anc{\lone} (\face{\ltwo}{\coarseIdxone}) = \anc{\l} (\face{\ltwo}{\coarseIdxone})$ for $\l \leq \lone \leq \ltwo$, and $\anc{\lone}(\face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone}) = \face{\lone}{\coarseIdxone}$ is the identity. Refining and then coarsening returns the argument, i.e. $\anc{\l}(\Aop{}{\l}{\lone} \, \simplexsubsetTwo{\l}) = \simplexsubsetTwo{\l}$, because quadrisection is injective on face sets. The other composition does not, since $\anc{\l}$ keeps a coarse face as soon as only one of its children is present, and that is precisely where the face ancestor differs from the ancestor region $\ancestorPatch{\cdot}$ of Definition~\ref{def:ancestor_patch}. The two agree wherever $\ancestorPatch{\cdot}$ is defined,
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:ancestor_patch_is_anc}
    \ancestorPatch{\simplexsubsetTwo{\l+1}} \; = \; \anc{\l} ( \simplexsubsetTwo{\l+1} ) \qquad \text{for quadrisection-closed} \qquad \simplexsubsetTwo{\l+1}.
\end{equation}
We keep both, because $\ancestorPatch{\cdot}$ carries that hypothesis in its notation, which is what makes the nesting condition of Definition~\ref{def:domseq} readable, while $\anc{\l}$ is what the neighbourhoods below need since a mesh star is not quadrisection-closed.

\begin{lemma}[Ancestral closure of a domain sequence]
    \label{lemma:ancestral_closure}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ with regions $\M_\lone$ and let $\lzero \leq \l < \lone \leq \L$. Every face of $\M_\lone$ has its level-$\l$ ancestor refined beyond level $\l$,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:ancestral_closure}
        \anc{\l} \big( \simplexsubsetTwo{\lone} \big) \; \subseteq \; \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}} \; \subseteq \; \M_\l \qquad \text{for every} \qquad \simplexsubsetTwo{\lone} \subseteq \M_\lone.
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The face ancestor operator acts facewise by Definition~\ref{def:face_ancestor}, so it suffices to walk a single face $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \in \simplexsubsetTwo{\lone} \subseteq \M_\lone$ down one level at a time. Let $\lzero \leq \l < \lone$ and suppose $\anc{\l+1}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) \in \M_{\l+1}$. Since $\l \geq \lzero$, Definition~\ref{def:domseq} makes $\M_{\l+1}$ quadrisection-closed, that is $\M_{\l+1} = \Aop{}{\l}{\l+1}(\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}})$, so $\anc{\l+1}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone})$ is a child of a face of $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$, which can only be its parent $\anc{\l}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone})$. The nesting condition of the same definition carries that parent on into $\M_\l$. This is the assertion on level $\l$ and the supposition one level further down, and it starts at $\l = \lone \! - \! 1$ because $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \in \M_\lone$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Covering face]
    \label{def:covering_face}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs$ and let $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \in \F_{\lone}(\T_\lone)$ be a face on level $\lone$. The \emph{covering face} of $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}$ is the one face ancestor of $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}$ that is a leaf face,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:covering_face}
        \coveringFace \big( \face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \big) \; \coloneq \; \anc{\l} \big( \face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \big) \qquad \text{for the level } \l \text{ with } \; \anc{\l} \big( \face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \big) \, \in \, \leafMesh(\domseq[\L]) .
    \end{equation}
    At most one level meets that condition, and we set $\coveringFace(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) \coloneq \varnothing$ when none does. The next two results show both, and identify the faces for which no level qualifies.
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[tb]

\node[lvl] at (-3.5, \rowB) {$\l = 1$};

\node[leafn] (c3) at ({\treeone + 0.5*\dxB}, \rowB) {};

% children of c1 lie in M_L; children of the leaf c4 exist in T_L only

}

\draw[walk] (h2) to[bend left=20] (c4);

\node[tag, anchor=west, align=left] at ($(p.east) + (0.18,0)$)
      {$\lstar = \lzero = \coveringFace \big( \face{1}{\fineIdxtwo} \big)$};

Solid nodes are the faces of the regions $\M_\l$, dashed nodes are faces that exist in the uniform mesh $\T_\l$ but that are outside the regions $\M_\l$ of the domain sequence. A leaf face is a solid node colored in blue, while purple marks a face on level $\l$ refined beyond its own level and thus in $\ancestorPatch{\M_\l+1}$. The four children of a solid node are always present together, because $\M_{\l+1}$ is quadrisection-closed. A leaf has no children in the forest, but the faces below it exist in $\T_\l$ all the way to $\L$. Those are drawn only where a walk starts and are marked by dots when omitted.
Panel~(a) shows the ancestor walk of Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestor_walk_finest} from a face of the finest level using red arrows. It meets the three regimes of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} in order and crosses at $\lstar = 1$. The node marked $\coveringFace = \varnothing$ is the other branch of Corollary~\ref{cor:ancestor_walk}. It is refined beyond its own level, so the walk that starts there climbs through interior nodes alone and never meets a leaf.
In Panel~(b) the root itself is the leaf, so $\lstar = \lzero$ and the third regime of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} is empty. This walk also starts at a coarser level than $\L$, which is Corollary~\ref{cor:ancestor_walk}$(i)$. Ancestry never leaves the tree it starts in, which is why the two trees can be read independently and why the crossing level is a property of one coarse face at a time.}
\label{fig:ancestor_walk}
\end{figure}

Read from the fine end at level $\L$, the ancestors of a single face therefore pass through three regimes, and the two results above are exactly what forbids a fourth. Figure~\ref{fig:ancestor_walk} draws all of this on the quadtree of the faces as they are quadrisected level by level. The split by level is a statement about ancestry, and ancestry provides the tree. We first walk the whole hierarchy, starting from the finest level $\L$, where the crossing between the regimes always exists. Afterwards we ask what survives of the split when the walk starts at a coarser level instead.

\begin{lemma}[The ancestor walk at the finest level]
    \label{lemma:ancestor_walk_finest}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ with regions $\M_\l$ and let $\face{\L}{\fineIdxone} \in \F_\L(\T_\L)$ be a face of the finest mesh. Then $\face{\L}{\fineIdxone}$ has exactly one ancestor that is a leaf face of $\domseq[\L]$, namely its covering face
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:covering_face_at_finest}
        \coveringFace \big( \face{\L}{\fineIdxone} \big) \; = \; \anc{\lstar} \big( \face{\L}{\fineIdxone} \big) \; \in \; \leafF{\lstar}(\domseq[\L]) \qquad \text{on a level} \qquad \lzero \leq \lstar \leq \L ,
    \end{equation}
    and its ancestors split by level into
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:ancestor_walk}
        \anc{\l} \big( \face{\L}{\fineIdxone} \big) \; \begin{cases}
            \notin \; \M_\l & \text{for} \quad \lzero \leq \lstar < \l \leq \L , \\[2pt]
            \in \; \leafF{\lstar}(\domseq[\L]) & \text{for} \quad \lzero \leq \lstar = \l \leq \L , \\[2pt]
            \in \; \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}} \subseteq \M_{\l} & \text{for} \quad \lzero \leq \l < \lstar \leq \L .
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Write $f_\l \coloneq \anc{\l}(\face{\L}{\fineIdxone})$ for the ancestor on level $\l$ and collect the levels on which the walk is inside the domain sequence,
    \begin{equation*}
        S \; \coloneq \; \big\{ \, \lzero \leq \l \leq \L \; \colon \;\; f_\l \in \M_\l \, \big\} .
    \end{equation*}
    The region $\M_\lzero = \F_\lzero(\T_\lzero)$ contains every level-$\lzero$ face by Definition~\ref{def:domseq}, so $\lzero \in S$ and $\lstar \coloneq \max (S)$ exists. Three steps show that this level carries the unique leaf ancestor, which is the assertion.

    \emph{Step 1. $S = \{ \lzero, \, \dots, \, \lstar \}$ is an interval.} Take any $\l < \lstar$. Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestral_closure} applied to $\{ f_\lstar \} \subseteq \M_\lstar$ places $f_\l$ in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}} \subseteq \M_\l$, so $\l \in S$. Above $\lstar$ the definition of the maximum gives $f_\l \notin \M_\l$. These are the third and the first row of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk}.

    \emph{Step 2. The ancestor $f_\lstar$ is a leaf face.} Suppose it is not. It lies in $\M_\lstar$, so the splitting~\eqref{eq:leaf_ancestor_split} puts it in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\lstar+1}}$ and all four of its children lie in $\M_{\lstar+1}$. Were $\lstar$ smaller than $\L$, one of those children would be $f_{\lstar+1}$, which puts $\lstar \! + \! 1$ into $S$ and contradicts maximality. Hence $\lstar = \L$ and $f_\L \in \ancestorPatch{\M_{\L+1}}$, which is empty because Definition~\ref{def:domseq} sets $\M_{\L+1} = \varnothing$. This is the second row of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk}, and it is the only step that uses the finest level.

    \emph{Step 3. No other ancestor is a leaf face.} A leaf face lies in the region of its level by Definition~\ref{def:leaf_faces}, which rules out every level above $\lstar$ by Step~1. Below $\lstar$ the ancestor lies in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$, and~\eqref{eq:leaf_ancestor_split} makes that half of $\M_\l$ disjoint from $\leafF{\l}(\domseq[\L])$.

    Steps~2 and~3 together with Definition~\ref{def:covering_face} give~\eqref{eq:covering_face_at_finest}, and all three steps give~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk}.
\end{proof}

Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestor_walk_finest} implies that the escape clause $\coveringFace(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) \coloneq \varnothing$ of Definition~\ref{def:covering_face} cannot fire at the finest level $\L$. This is the one level at which the covering face is always defined, and it is why the constructions below read a neighbourhood at level $\L$ and not at the level of the vertex. Starting the walk from a coarser face on level $\lone$ may or may not cross $\lstar$ as the next corollary shows.

\begin{corollary}[The ancestor walk from a coarser level]
    \label{cor:ancestor_walk}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ with regions $\M_\l$ and let $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \in \F_\lone(\T_\lone)$ with $\lzero \leq \lone \leq \L$. It holds that
    \begin{itemize}
        \item if $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \notin \ancestorPatch{\M_{\lone+1}}$, i.e. it is not refined beyond level $\lone$, then
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[$(i)$] $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}$ has exactly one ancestor $\anc{\lstar}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) = \coveringFace(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) \in \leafF{\lstar}(\domseq[\L])$ on a level $\lzero \leq \lstar \leq \lone$ that is also a leaf face,
            \item[$(ii)$] and its ancestors $\anc{\l}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone})$ split by level as in~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk}, with $\L$ replaced by $\lone$ throughout;
        \end{itemize}
        \item if $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \in \ancestorPatch{\M_{\lone+1}}$, i.e. it is refined beyond level $\lone$, then
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[$(i')$] $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}$ has no covering face, i.e. $\coveringFace(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) = \varnothing$,
            \item[$(ii')$] and $\anc{\l}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) \in \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}} \subseteq \M_\l$ for all $\lzero \leq \l \leq \lone$.
        \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Pick any descendant $\face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo} \in \Aop{}{\lone}{\L}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone})$ on the finest level and let $\lstar$ be the level that Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestor_walk_finest} gives for it. Ancestry composes, so $\anc{\l}(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) = \anc{\l}(\face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo})$ for all $\lzero \leq \l \leq \lone$ and the walk of $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}$ is the tail of the walk of $\face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo}$. Everything follows from whether that tail still reaches the crossing at $\lstar$.

    \emph{The tail reaches it exactly when $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone} \notin \ancestorPatch{\M_{\lone+1}}$.} Read~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} at $\l = \lone$. Its third row is the condition $\lone < \lstar$ and places $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}$ in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\lone+1}}$. The other two rows place it outside $\M_\lone$ or inside $\leafF{\lone}(\domseq[\L])$, and the nesting condition of Definition~\ref{def:domseq} and the splitting~\eqref{eq:leaf_ancestor_split} keep both apart from $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\lone+1}}$. The rows are exhaustive, so $\lstar > \lone$ holds exactly when $\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}$ is refined beyond its own level.

    \emph{The surviving branch.} Let $\lstar \leq \lone$. The tail carries all three rows of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} over the range $\lzero \leq \l \leq \lone$, which is~$(ii)$. It also contains the level $\lstar$, so it inherits the leaf ancestor of the lemma together with its uniqueness, and Definition~\ref{def:covering_face} names that ancestor the covering face. That is~$(i)$.

    \emph{The dying branch.} Let $\lstar > \lone$. Every level of the tail falls in the third row of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk}, which is~$(ii')$. The one leaf ancestor sits at $\lstar$ and thus outside the tail, so the walk from $\lone$ meets none and $\coveringFace(\face{\lone}{\fineIdxone}) = \varnothing$ by Definition~\ref{def:covering_face}, which is~$(i')$.

    Neither branch depends on the descendant we picked. The branch is fixed by the hypothesis through the equivalence above, and $\lstar$ is then fixed by the uniqueness in~$(i)$.
\end{proof}

Both branches of the corollary are drawn in Figure~\ref{fig:ancestor_walk}. The surviving one is the walk in Panel~(b), which starts at level $1$ and still reaches a crossing because the root of its tree is a leaf face. The dying one is the node marked $\coveringFace = \varnothing$ in Panel~(a), whose walk climbs through interior nodes alone.

\medskip

The rest of this subsection reads a vertex on more than one level, so we now fix what it means to speak of a vertex across the hierarchy. Loop subdivision retains every vertex of $\T_\l$ as a vertex of $\T_{\l+1}$ and inserts one new vertex per edge, see Section~\ref{subsec:subdiv_basis_functions}. This gives an injection
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:vertex_injection}
    \V_\l(\T_\l) \; \hookrightarrow \; \V_{\l+1}(\T_{\l+1}) , \qquad \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \; \longmapsto \; \vertex{\l+1}{\coarseIdxone} ,
\end{equation}
whose complement is precisely the set of vertices inserted on level $\l \! + \! 1$. This is the action of $\Aop{}{\l}{\lone}$ on vertices used in Section~\ref{subsec:selecting_active_dofs}, and unlike its action on faces it is single-valued: a face has four children, a vertex has one image. The identification is combinatorial and not geometric, since subdivision moves the position of a vertex on every level.

\subsubsection{Vertex classes and their stars}
\label{subsubsec:vertex_classes}

\begin{definition}[Vertex class and birth level]
    \label{def:vertex_class}
    Call two vertices of the hierarchy \emph{equivalent} when one is carried onto the other by a composition of the injections~\eqref{eq:vertex_injection}. A \emph{vertex class} $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ is an equivalence class of this relation, that is the orbit of a single vertex under refinement. Its \emph{birth level} $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ is the coarsest level carrying a representative so that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:vertex_class}
        \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \; = \; \big\{ \vertex{\lone}{\coarseIdxone} \; \colon \;\; \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \, \leq \, \lone \, \leq \, \L \big\} ,
    \end{equation}
    and we write $\vertex{\lone}{\coarseIdxone}$ for the representative of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ on level $\lone$, which exists exactly for $\lone \geq \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$. We denote the set of all vertex classes of a mesh hierarchy by $\vertexClasses(\T_\L)$.
\end{definition}

Every class has one representative on each level from its birth level up to $\L$ and none below. The classes of birth level $\lzero$ are the vertices of the control mesh, and a class of birth level $\l > \lzero$ is the vertex that the quadrisection of level $\l \! - \! 1$ inserts on one of its edges.

The number of vertex classes $\vertexClasses(\T_\L)$ is exactly the number of vertices of level $\L$ because every vertex $\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone}$ is a representative of its vertex class $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$. For that reason, we will often state properties and operators on $\vertexClasses(\T_\L)$ in terms of $\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone}$.

Given a domain sequence $\domseq[\L]$ we write
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:vertex_classes_of_domseq}
    \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L]) \; \coloneq \; \big\{ \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses \; \colon \;\; \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V_\l(\M_\l) \;\text{ for at least one level }\; \l \big\}
\end{equation}
for the classes that have a representative among the vertices~\eqref{eq:vertices_of_domseq} of the domain sequence. In general, $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ is a proper subset of $\vertexClasses(\T_\L)$, because a class born above level $\lzero$ does not need to be part of any region of $\domseq[\L]$. Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} identifies the levels on which a class of $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ has such a representative.

\begin{definition}[Class star]
    \label{def:star_ancestry}
    Let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses$ be a vertex class with level-$L$ representative $\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone}$ and let $\l \in \{ \lzero, \, \dots, \, \L \}$. The \emph{class star} of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ on level $\l$ is the set
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:star_ancestry}
        \FVanc{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; \coloneq \; \anc{\l} \big( \FVl{\L} ( \vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone} ) \big)
    \end{equation}
    of those level-$\l$ faces that have a child in the mesh star $\FVl{\L}(\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone})$ of $\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone}$.
\end{definition}

On the levels $\l \geq \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ at which the class representative $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ exists, the class star coincides with the mesh star, i.e.
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:star_ancestry_is_star}
    \FVanc{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; = \; \FVl{\l} \big( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \big), \qquad \l \geq \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}),
\end{equation}
What the star ancestry adds are the coarser levels $\l < \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$, at which $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ has no representative and $\FVl{\l}$ is undefined. For Loop subdivision, the class star on level $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \! - \! 1$ contains the two faces sharing the edge on which $\vertex{\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})}{\coarseIdxone}$ will be created. On the levels below $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \! - \! 1$, the class star contains the ancestors of those two faces, which may coincide in a single face. Thus, the class star satisfies the following nestedness property:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:star_ancestry_nested}
    \FVanc{\l+1} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) \; \subseteq \; \Aop{}{\l}{\l+1} \big( \FVanc{\l} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) \big) \qquad \text{for} \qquad \lzero \leq \l < \L.
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}[Leaf star]
    \label{def:leaf_star}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ and let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses(\T_\L)$ be a vertex class with finest representative $\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone}$. The \emph{leaf star} $\FVleaf{} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big)$ is the collection of all leaf faces of $\domseq[\L]$ covering the mesh star $\FVl{\L}(\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone})$, i.e.
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:leaf_star}
        \FVleaf{} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; \coloneq \; \big\{ \coveringFace \big( \face{\L}{\fineIdxone} \big) \; \colon \;\; \face{\L}{\fineIdxone} \in \FVl{\L} \big( \vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \big\}.
    \end{equation}
    Note that the covering face exists for every $\face{\L}{\fineIdxone}$ because of Equation~\eqref{eq:covering_face_at_finest}. We add a level subscript to the leaf star to indicate its \emph{level-$\l$ slice}
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:leaf_star_slice}
        \FVleaf{\l} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) \; \coloneq \; \FVleaf{} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) \cap \F_\l(\T_\l) \;\; \subseteq \;\; \leafF{\l}(\domseq[\L]).
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}

The class star of Definition~\ref{def:star_ancestry} and the leaf star of Definition~\ref{def:leaf_star} are therefore the same construction reading two different ways to move across the hierarchy. Both start from the mesh star $\FVl{\L}(\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone})$ at the finest level. The class star climbs uniformly to a prescribed level $\l$ using the ancestor operator $\anc{\l}$ of Definition~\ref{def:face_ancestor}. In contrast, the leaf star collects the leaf faces covering the mesh star at whichever level the domain sequence happens them and is thus fully determined by the domain sequence.

% exists for this figure: the leaf mesh alone carries neither the level-$\l$

Reading across, the fan is the whole mesh star below $\lmin$, a proper part of it on the levels of the level span, and empty from $\lmax$ on, while the leaf star fills in behind it by splitting off one slice per level as in~\eqref{eq:star_splits}.}

By Definition~\ref{def:leaf_star}, a leaf face of the leaf star covers a face of $\FVl{\L}(\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone})$ and thus it holds that
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:leaf_star_in_ancestry}
    \FVleaf{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; \subseteq \; \FVanc{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big).
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}[Coarsest and finest level of a leaf star]
    \label{def:level_range}
    Let $\FVleaf{}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ be the leaf star of the vertex class $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$. We write
    \begin{alignat}{2}
        \label{eq:level_range}
        \lminIdx{\coarseIdxone} &= \lmin ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) &&\coloneq \min \{ \l \; \colon \; \FVleaf{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \cap \F_\l(\T_\l) \neq \varnothing \}, \\
        \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} &= \lmax ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) &&\coloneq \max \{ \l \; \colon \; \FVleaf{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \cap \F_\l(\T_\l) \neq \varnothing \}
    \end{alignat}
    for the coarsest and the finest level of faces contained in the leaf star of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$. Finally, we define the \emph{level span} $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone}$ of the leaf star by $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} \coloneq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} - \lminIdx{\coarseIdxone}$.
\end{definition}

The class star, the leaf star and the refinement fan of Section~\ref{subsec:subdiv_partition} are three readings of one neighbourhood, and which of them a level carries is decided by that level alone. Applying the ancestor walk of Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestor_walk_finest} to every face of the mesh star on level $\L$ at once puts all three into a single statement, which closes this section.

\subsubsection{The class star level by level}
\label{subsubsec:level_split}

\begin{proposition}[The class star split by level]
    \label{proposition:class_star_levels}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ with regions $\M_\l$ and let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ have leaf star $\FVleaf{}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ of coarsest level $\lmin$ and finest level $\lmax$ as in Definition~\ref{def:level_range}. Then the part of the class star that is refined beyond its own level is
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:class_star_levels}
        \FVanc{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \cap \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}} \; \begin{cases}
            = \; \FVanc{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) & \text{for} \quad \lzero \leq \l < \lmin , \\[2pt]
            \subsetneq \; \FVanc{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \quad \text{and} \quad \neq \; \varnothing & \text{for} \quad \lmin \leq \l < \lmax , \\[2pt]
            = \; \varnothing & \text{for} \quad \lmax \leq \l \leq \L ,
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    and below $\lmin$ the domain sequence already contains all children of the class star,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:class_star_children}
        \Aop{}{\l}{\l+1} \big( \FVanc{\l} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) \big) \; \subseteq \; \M_{\l+1} \qquad \text{for all} \qquad \lzero \leq \l < \lmin.
    \end{equation}
    On the levels $\l \geq \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ at which the class has a representative, the class star is the mesh star by~\eqref{eq:star_ancestry_is_star} and the intersection in~\eqref{eq:class_star_levels} is the refinement fan $\refFan{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ of Definition~\ref{def:refinement_fan}. The three cases then read that the fan is the full mesh star below $\lmin$, a non-empty proper subset of it on the levels $\lmin \leq \l < \lmax$, and empty from $\lmax$ on. In particular $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ is refinement-exterior in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:refinement_exterior_vertex} exactly on the levels $\l \geq \lmax$. The third case is never vacuous, because $\lmax \leq \L$ by Definition~\ref{def:level_range} and the fan on the finest level is empty for every vertex anyway. Above $\lmax$ the class star misses the region $\M_\l$ altogether, so the class has a representative in $\V(\domseq[\L])$ exactly on the levels $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \leq \l \leq \lmax$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo} \in \FVl{\L}(\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone})$ be a face of the mesh star at the finest level and let $\lstar(\face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo})$ be the level Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestor_walk_finest} gives for it. Its covering face lies in the leaf star by Definition~\ref{def:leaf_star}, so
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:crossing_level_range}
        \lmin \; \leq \; \lstar \big( \face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo} \big) \; \leq \; \lmax \qquad \text{for every} \qquad \face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo} \in \FVl{\L}(\vertex{\L}{\coarseIdxone}),
    \end{equation}
    and both bounds are attained by Definition~\ref{def:level_range}. Every face of the class star on level $\l$ is $\anc{\l}(\face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo})$ for such a face by Definition~\ref{def:star_ancestry}, and which row of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} it falls into is decided by comparing $\l$ with $\lstar(\face{\L}{\fineIdxtwo})$, cf.\ Figure~\ref{fig:ancestor_walk}. The three cases are the three ways the level $\l$ can sit against the range~\eqref{eq:crossing_level_range}.

    \emph{Below $\lmin$.} Every face of the mesh star has $\l < \lmin \leq \lstar$, so every face of the class star falls in the third row of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} and lies in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$. That is the first case, and a face of $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$ has all four of its children in $\M_{\l+1}$, which is~\eqref{eq:class_star_children}.

    \emph{Inside the level span.} Let $\lmin \leq \l < \lmax$. A face of the mesh star with $\lstar = \lmax > \l$ exists and falls in the third row, so its level-$\l$ ancestor lies in the intersection and the intersection is not empty. A face with $\lstar = \lmin \leq \l$ exists as well and falls in the first or the second row, so its level-$\l$ ancestor lies outside $\M_\l$ or is a leaf face, and the splitting~\eqref{eq:leaf_ancestor_split} keeps both apart from $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$. That ancestor is in the class star and not in the intersection, so the inclusion is proper.

    \emph{From $\lmax$ on.} Every face of the mesh star has $\lstar \leq \lmax \leq \l$, so no face of the class star falls in the third row of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} and none of them lies in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$. For $\l > \lmax$ the inequality is strict, the first row applies to every face of the mesh star, and the whole class star lies outside $\M_\l$. The faces of $\M_\l$ containing $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ are exactly $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \cap \M_\l$ by~\eqref{eq:star_ancestry_is_star}, so $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \notin \V_\l(\M_\l)$ there, whereas on $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \leq \l \leq \lmax$ the level-$\l$ ancestor of a face of the mesh star with $\lstar = \lmax \geq \l$ lies in $\M_\l$ by the second or the third row.
\end{proof}

Read at one fixed level instead of across levels, the same walk splits the mesh star itself. The proof sorts the faces of $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ with $\l \geq \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ by the row of~\eqref{eq:ancestor_walk} their crossing level puts them in, and the rows are exhaustive and disjoint because $\lstar$ is unique whenever it exists. They are the faces refined beyond $\l$, which form the refinement fan $\refFan{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$, the faces that are their own covering face, which form the slice $\FVleaf{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$, and the strict descendants of the coarser leaf faces $\FVleaf{\lstar}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$, $\lstar < \l$. Removing the third group leaves the first two,
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:star_splits}
    \FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}) \, \setminus \, \bigsqcup\limits_{\lstar = \lzero}^{\l-1} \; \Aop{}{\lstar}{\l} \, \big( \FVleaf{\lstar} (\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \big) \; = \; \refFan{\l} \big( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; \sqcup \;  \FVleaf{\l} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \qquad \forall \l \; \geq \; \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) .
\end{equation}

Figure~\ref{fig:leaf_star_levels} follows a single vertex class through~\eqref{eq:class_star_levels} and~\eqref{eq:star_splits} together. Read across the row the refinement fan empties into the leaf star level by level by splitting off the level-$\l$ slice $\FVleaf{\l} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} )$, and the level at which it is empty is $\lmax$.

\smallskip

Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} is the central result of this section and summarises the different cases an algorithm could meet and needs to avoid to guarantee admissibility of domain sequences according to Definition~\ref{def:domseq}. The refinement operators introduced in the next section generally aim to close the level span $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} = \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} - \lminIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ by raising both of them to a prescribed target level. We call this process \emph{homogenisation} of the class star because the proposition shows that $\lminIdx{\coarseIdxone} = \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ implies that the leaf star of $\vertexClasses{\coarseIdxone}$ is entirely on level $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$.

The third case of Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} names the level at which a vertex first becomes refinement-exterior as the finest level $\lmax$ of its leaf star, which Section~\ref{subsec:subdiv_partition} leaves unnamed and Figure~\ref{fig:adm_exterior} colours its vertices by. The second case says that a partial refinement fan occurs exactly between the levels of the level span of Definition~\ref{def:level_range}, and so that these are the only levels at which the vertex can be a pinch in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:pinch_vertex}.

The closing sentence of Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} also decides which classes the domain sequence carries at all. It turns the membership~\eqref{eq:vertex_classes_of_domseq} into an inequality between two levels.

\begin{corollary}[The vertex classes a domain sequence carries]
    \label{cor:birth_below_lmax}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$. Definition~\ref{def:leaf_star} reads the leaf star at every $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses$, so its finest level $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ is defined for every vertex class in the entire mesh hierarchy. A vertex class is a member of the domain sequence classes $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ exactly when its leaf star reaches its own birth level, i.e.
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:birth_below_lmax}
        \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L]) \; = \; \big\{ \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses \; \colon \;\; \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \, \leq \, \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} \big\} .
    \end{equation}
    Such a class has a representative $\vertex{\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V_{\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}}(\M_{\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}})$ on the level $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ itself.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$. Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} gives a representative in $\V(\domseq[\L])$ exactly on the levels $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \leq \l \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$, and~\eqref{eq:vertex_classes_of_domseq} supplies at least one such level. That range is therefore non-empty, which is the inequality.

    For the converse let $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ and write $\l \coloneq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$. Definition~\ref{def:level_range} gives a leaf face $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone} \in \FVleaf{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$, and a leaf face of $\domseq[\L]$ lies in $\M_\l$. It also lies in $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ by~\eqref{eq:leaf_star_in_ancestry}, and that is the mesh star $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ by~\eqref{eq:star_ancestry_is_star} because $\l \geq \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$. So $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ carries $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$, which puts $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ in $\V_\l(\M_\l)$ and $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ in $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Interactions of vertex classes and their stars}
\label{subsubsec:two_classes}

The three neighbourhoods so far were read at one vertex class. Read at two classes, they also say when they decouple, and the refinement operators of Section~\ref{subsec:refinement_operators} need to know how far one class can reach into the neighbourhood of another. The answer comes in two parts. The first measures how fast two classes separate under refinement and reads the mesh hierarchy alone. The second names the level at which they part and closes with the one statement that adds the domain sequence.

\begin{lemma}[Birth levels on an edge]
    \label{lemma:edge_birth_levels}
    Let $\lzero < \l \leq \L$ and let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ and $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ be the two vertex classes whose level-$\l$ representatives $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ and $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ are the endpoints of an edge of $\T_\l$. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:edge_birth_levels}
        \max \big( \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}), \, \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) \big) \; = \; \l .
    \end{equation}
    No edge of $\T_\l$ ever joins the representatives of two classes that are both born below $\l$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Every vertex of $\T_\l$ is either a corner of a face of $\T_{\l-1}$ or the midpoint of one of its edges. Quadrisection creates these midpoints on level $\l$, so their classes are born there. Every edge of $\T_\l$ lies in a face of $\T_\l$, and that face is one of the four children of a face of $\T_{\l-1}$. Each of those children carries at most one of the three corners of its parent, the three corner children one each and the middle child none. No face of $\T_\l$ therefore holds two corners, and no edge of $\T_\l$ joins two.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Distances double under quadrisection]
    \label{lemma:distance_doubling}
    Write $\dist{\l}$ for the graph distance in the edge graph of $\T_\l$. Let $\lzero \leq \l \leq \L$, let $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ and $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ be vertices of $\T_\l$, and let $0 \leq k \leq \L - \l$. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:distance_doubling}
        \dist{\l+k} \big( \vertex{\l+k}{\coarseIdxone}, \vertex{\l+k}{\coarseIdxtwo} \big) \; = \; 2^k \; \dist{\l} \big( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}, \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo} \big) .
    \end{equation}
    Two vertices never come closer as the mesh is refined, and their separation grows by the same factor as the mesh.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    It is enough to treat $k = 1$ and iterate. Write $d$ for the distance of the two vertices on level $\l$. Quadrisection splits every edge into two. Subdividing a shortest path between them therefore gives a path of length $2d$ on level $\l \! + \! 1$, and the distance there is at most $2d$. A fine path cannot outperform a coarse one because inside each face the level-$\l \! + \! 1$ mesh is a scaled copy of its parent and crossing between two of its corners costs two fine edges. Cutting across the interiors of faces therefore saves nothing anywhere along the way.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[What adjacency on one level costs on the next]
    \label{cor:adjacent_classes}
    Let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \neq \vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ be vertex classes whose level-$\l$ representatives are adjacent, i.e. they are the endpoints of an edge of $\T_\l$ with $\lzero \leq \l < \L$. Then
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=3pt, itemsep=-2pt]
        \item[$(i)$] one of the two classes is born on level $\l$,
        \item[$(ii)$] on level $\l \! + \! 1$ their two mesh stars share exactly one vertex, the midpoint of their shared edge, and no face,
        \item[$(iii)$] on every level $\l \! + \! 2$ and above their mesh stars are disjoint.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Clause~$(i)$ is Lemma~\ref{lemma:edge_birth_levels}. For the other two, the two representatives are at distance~$2^k$ on level $\l \! + \! k$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:distance_doubling}. On level $\l \! + \! 1$ that distance is two, so the stars share no face and a shared vertex is a common neighbour. Both representatives are older than level $\l \! + \! 1$, so each of their neighbours there is a midpoint of an edge of $\T_\l$ at them by Lemma~\ref{lemma:edge_birth_levels}, and the only edge of $\T_\l$ at both is the one they span. Its midpoint is the one shared vertex, which is~$(ii)$. On level $\l \! + \! k$ with $k \geq 2$ the distance is at least four, so the two are not at distance at most two and the stars share nothing, which is~$(iii)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Reach of a vertex class]
    \label{lemma:class_reach}
    Let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \neq \vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ be distinct vertex classes and let $\l$ be a level at which both representatives exist and the mesh stars $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ and $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo})$ share a vertex. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:class_reach}
        \max \big( \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}), \, \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) \big) \; \leq \; \l \; \leq \; \max \big( \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}), \, \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) \big) \; + \; 1 ,
    \end{equation}
    so at most two levels carry a shared vertex. The upper bound holds with equality only if the shared vertex is born on level $\l$ and $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ and $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ are the two endpoints of the edge of $\T_{\l-1}$ that it subdivides. In particular both classes are then born strictly below $\l$ and are adjacent on level $\l \! - \! 1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Write $\lone \coloneq \max ( \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}), \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) )$ for the later of the two birth levels. The lower bound is the hypothesis, since a class has a representative only from its birth level on and both representatives exist on level $\l$. Both classes have a representative on level $\lone$, and their distance $d$ there is at least one because they are distinct. Two mesh stars share a vertex exactly when their vertices are at distance at most two, so the hypothesis and Lemma~\ref{lemma:distance_doubling} give $2^{\l - \lone} d = \dist{\l} ( \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}, \vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo} ) \leq 2$, which forces $\l \leq \lone \! + \! 1$.

    Equality needs $d = 1$, so the two representatives are adjacent on level $\lone = \l \! - \! 1$, and clause~$(ii)$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:adjacent_classes} identifies the shared vertex as the midpoint of the edge they span.
\end{proof}

The results so far measure the separation of two classes level by level. The refinement operators of Section~\ref{subsec:refinement_operators} need the level at which it happens as a single number instead.

The levels on which the class stars of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ and $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ share a face are downward closed, since the ancestor of a common face is a common face and the ancestor operator $\anc{}$ composes. We may therefore write $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ for the finest level at which the two classes still share a face. We denote this \emph{meeting level} by
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:meeting_level}
    \lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} \; \coloneq \; \max \big\{ \l \; \colon \;\; \FVanc{\l} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} ) \cap \FVanc{\l} ( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo} ) \neq \varnothing \big\},
\end{equation}
and set $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} = - 1$ if the two never share a face, not even on level $\lzero$. On the levels $\l$ at which both classes have a representative, sharing a face means that $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ and $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ lie in a common face of $\T_\l$ by~\eqref{eq:star_ancestry_is_star}, so there $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ is the finest level at which the two representatives are still adjacent. Like the class star itself, this reads the mesh hierarchy alone and no domain sequence.

Both classes have a representative on every level from the later of their two birth levels on, and there sharing a face means that the two representatives are adjacent by~\eqref{eq:star_ancestry_is_star}, that is at distance one. Their distance on that later birth level is at least one because the classes are distinct, so Lemma~\ref{lemma:distance_doubling} puts it at two or more on every level above it. Adjacency is therefore available on the later birth level and on no level above, which with the downward closure gives
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:meeting_level_bound}
    \lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} \; \leq \; \max \big( \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}), \, \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) \big) \quad \text{for} \quad \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \neq \vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo},
\end{equation}
with equality exactly when the two representatives are adjacent on level $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo}$. Two distinct classes therefore decouple no later than the birth of the later born one, and the meeting level never has to be computed level by level. As a special case, $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} = \L$ forces one of the two classes to be born on the finest level, so the only pairs that never decouple are those. Corollary~\ref{cor:graded_quadrisection_leafstar} is where this is used.

\begin{lemma}[Two classes part inside their own leaf stars]
    \label{lemma:meeting_level_leafstar}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ and let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \neq \vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ be two distinct vertex classes of $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:meeting_level_leafstar}
        \lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} \; \leq \; \min \big( \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}, \, \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxtwo} \big).
    \end{equation}
    Two classes of a domain sequence stop sharing a face no later than the finest level either of their leaf stars carries.
\end{lemma}

Before we prove the lemma, we want to explain why it has content. At first sight~\eqref{eq:meeting_level_leafstar} looks like it follows from Definition~\ref{def:level_range} of the level span alone. The leaf star of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ does indeed stop at $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$. However, the meeting level~\eqref{eq:meeting_level} measures the class star $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$, and that one does not stop. It is defined on every level $\l$ and equals the full mesh star $\FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ from the birth level on by~\eqref{eq:star_ancestry_is_star}. Above $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} says that it lies outside the region $\M_\l$, not that it is empty. So~\eqref{eq:meeting_level_leafstar} bounds a quantity of the mesh hierarchy alone by two levels that depend on the domain sequence.

\begin{proof}
    By symmetry it is enough to prove $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$. Assume $\l \coloneq \lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} > \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ and let $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone} \in \FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \cap \FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo})$ be a shared face.

    The proof constructs a contradiction, where one face of the class star of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ needs to be both refined beyond its own level and not refined beyond it at the same time. Steps~1 and~3 place two faces in the class star $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo})$, one outside $\M_\l$ and one inside it. Step~2 keeps that class star small, so the two are forced next to each other, and Step~4 fixes where $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ sits inside its parent. Three positions remain and each one is ruled out by~\eqref{eq:meeting_leafstar_fan}. Each vertex class is used once. Step~2 spends the membership of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ in $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ and Step~3 that of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$.

    \emph{Step 1 $($Nothing at $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ is refined from level $\l \! - \! 1$ on$)$.} Since $\l > \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$, the third case of Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} applies on both of the levels $\l \! - \! 1$ and $\l$ and gives
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:meeting_leafstar_fan}
        \FVanc{\lone} \big( \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \big) \, \cap \, \ancestorPatch{\M_{\lone+1}} \; = \; \varnothing \qquad \text{for} \qquad \lone \, \in \, \{ \l \! - \! 1, \, \l \} .
    \end{equation}
    The same proposition puts the whole class star $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ outside $\M_\l$, so
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:meeting_leafstar_step}
        \face{\l}{\fineIdxone} \; \notin \; \M_\l .
    \end{equation}

    \emph{Step 2 $($The class $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ is born on level $\l$ or above$)$.} Corollary~\ref{cor:birth_below_lmax} gives $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} < \l$. A meeting on level $\l$ needs one of the two classes born on level $\l$ by~\eqref{eq:meeting_level_bound}, and $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ is born strictly below it, so
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:meeting_leafstar_birth}
        b_\coarseIdxtwo \; \coloneq \; \birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) \; \geq \; \l .
    \end{equation}

    \emph{Step 3 $($The class star of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ contains a face of $\M_\l$$)$.} Corollary~\ref{cor:birth_below_lmax} gives $\birthLevel(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxtwo}$, so Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} puts a representative of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ on its own birth level and some face $\face{b_\coarseIdxtwo}{\fineIdxtwo} \in \M_{b_\coarseIdxtwo}$ carries $\vertex{b_\coarseIdxtwo}{\coarseIdxtwo}$. Write $\face{\l}{\fineIdxtwo}$ for its level-$\l$ ancestor. It lies in $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo})$ because $\anc{}$ composes, and it lies in $\M_\l$, by Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestral_closure} when $b_\coarseIdxtwo > \l$ and because it is $\face{b_\coarseIdxtwo}{\fineIdxtwo}$ itself when $b_\coarseIdxtwo = \l$. By~\eqref{eq:meeting_leafstar_step} it is not $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$.

    \emph{Step 4 $($The shared face is the corner child of its parent at $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$.} The representative $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ exists by Step~2 and $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}) = \FVl{\l}(\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone})$ by~\eqref{eq:star_ancestry_is_star}, so $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ carries $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$. Also $\l > \lzero$, so $\M_\l$ is quadrisection-closed and $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ has a parent. No edge of $\T_\l$ joins two vertices born below $\l$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:edge_birth_levels}, so a face of $\T_\l$ carries at most one of them and $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ is the corner child at $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$.

    It remains to place $\face{\l}{\fineIdxtwo}$ against $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$, and~\eqref{eq:meeting_leafstar_birth} leaves two shapes for the class star that holds them both.

    \emph{Case $b_\coarseIdxtwo > \l$.} The representative $\vertex{b_\coarseIdxtwo}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ is the midpoint of an edge of $\T_{b_\coarseIdxtwo-1}$, so $\FVanc{b_\coarseIdxtwo-1}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo})$ is the set of at most two faces of $\T_{b_\coarseIdxtwo-1}$ carrying that edge. Its image under $\anc{\l}$ is $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo})$, which therefore has at most two elements and by Steps~1 and~3 exactly two. Two adjacent faces with distinct ancestors have adjacent ancestors, so $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}) = \{ \face{\l}{\fineIdxone}, \face{\l}{\fineIdxtwo} \}$ are the two faces of $\T_\l$ carrying one edge $e$. There are two positions for $e$ against $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$.

    If $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ is an endpoint of $e$, then $\face{\l}{\fineIdxtwo}$ carries it too. It is refined beyond level $\l$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:ancestral_closure}, so the class star of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ on level $\l$ meets $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$, against~\eqref{eq:meeting_leafstar_fan}.

    Otherwise $e$ is the edge of $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ opposite $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$. That edge is interior to the parent by Step~4, so $\face{\l}{\fineIdxtwo}$ is the middle child of the same parent. Quadrisection-closedness then puts all four children of that parent into $\M_\l$, so the parent lies in $\ancestorPatch{\M_\l}$. It carries $\vertex{\l-1}{\coarseIdxone}$ because $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ is its corner child there, so the class star of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ on level $\l \! - \! 1$ meets $\ancestorPatch{\M_\l}$, against~\eqref{eq:meeting_leafstar_fan}.

    \emph{Case $b_\coarseIdxtwo = \l$.} Now $\face{\l}{\fineIdxone}$ carries both representatives. Its other two vertices are the midpoints of the two edges of its parent at $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ by Step~4, so $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo}$ is the midpoint of an edge $e'$ of $\T_{\l-1}$ with endpoint $\vertex{\l-1}{\coarseIdxone}$. The face $\face{\l}{\fineIdxtwo} \in \M_\l$ carries $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxtwo}$, so its parent carries $e'$ and hence $\vertex{\l-1}{\coarseIdxone}$. That parent lies in $\ancestorPatch{\M_\l}$, so the class star of $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ on level $\l \! - \! 1$ meets $\ancestorPatch{\M_\l}$, against~\eqref{eq:meeting_leafstar_fan}.

    Thus, all three possible positions contradict~\eqref{eq:meeting_leafstar_fan}, so the assumption $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} > \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ is false and $\lmeetIdx{\coarseIdxone}{\coarseIdxtwo} \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ follows.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Refinement operators}
\label{subsec:refinement_operators}
\label{subsec:operations_on_coverings}

The remainder of this section introduces the operators themselves, which enable the kinds of refinement that we will later need for the adaptive loop in Section~\ref{subsec:adaptive_loop}. The \emph{face quadrisection operator} $\quadriF(\domseq[\L], \simplexsubsetTwo{})$ refines a set of faces $\simplexsubsetTwo{}$, given one level at a time, by adding their children to the next level of the domain sequence and leaving everything else untouched.

\begin{definition}[Face quadrisection operator]
    \label{def:face_quadrisection_operator}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs$ be a domain sequence with regions $\M_\l$, $\l \in \{ \lzero, \, \dots \,, \L\}$. A \emph{marked family} for $\domseq[\L]$ is a disjoint union of face sets, one on each level below the finest,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:marked_face_family}
        \simplexsubsetTwo{} \; = \bigsqcup\limits_{\l = \lzero}^{\L-1} \simplexsubsetTwo{\l}, \qquad \simplexsubsetTwo{\lzero} \, \subseteq \, \F_\lzero(\T_\lzero), \qquad \simplexsubsetTwo{\l+1} \, \subseteq \, \M_{\l+1} \, \cup \, \Aop{}{\l}{\l+1} ( \simplexsubsetTwo{\l} )
    \end{equation}
    for $\lzero \leq \l \leq \L \! - \! 2$. The \emph{face quadrisection operator} $\quadriF \; \colon \; \alldomseqs_\L \times \powerset \big( \bigsqcup_{\l = \lzero}^{\L-1} \F_\l(\T_\l) \big) \; \longrightarrow \; \alldomseqs_\L$ is defined on the marked families of its first argument levelwise by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:def_face_quadrisection_operator}
        \quadriF \big( \domseq[\L], \, \simplexsubsetTwo{} \big) \; \coloneq \; \M_\lzero \, \sqcup \; \bigsqcup\limits_{\l = \lzero}^{\L-1} \, \big( \M_{\l+1} \, \cup \, \Aop{}{\l}{\l+1}(\simplexsubsetTwo{\l}) \big).
    \end{equation}
    We keep writing $\quadriF ( \domseq[\L], \simplexsubsetTwo{\l} )$ if only the level-$\l$ slice $\simplexsubsetTwo{\l}$ is non-empty.
\end{definition}

The recursion in~\eqref{eq:marked_face_family} is what the levelwise formula can digest, and it is weaker than asking $\simplexsubsetTwo{\l+1} \subseteq \M_{\l+1}$. A marked face on level $\l\!+\!1$ has to lie in the region of the \emph{output} on its level, and that region is $\M_{\l+1}$ enlarged by the children of the slice below. The narrower condition would forbid refining a face and then one of its children in a single application, which is precisely what the graded vertex quadrisection below needs. The base case carries no condition at all, since $\M_\lzero = \F_\lzero(\T_\lzero)$ by Definition~\ref{def:domseq}.

The result of the face quadrisection operator is again a domain sequence because each added set is quadrisection-closed with ancestor $\simplexsubsetTwo{\l}$, and so is a union of such sets. Writing $\M'_\l$ for the regions of $\quadriF ( \domseq[\L], \simplexsubsetTwo{\l} )$ the nesting condition of Definition~\ref{def:domseq} reads
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:face_quadrisection_nesting}
    \ancestorPatch{\M'_{\l+1}} \; = \; \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}} \, \cup \, \simplexsubsetTwo{\l} \; \subseteq \; \M_\l \, \cup \, \Aop{}{\l-1}{\l} \big( \simplexsubsetTwo{\l-1} \big) \; = \; \M'_\l
\end{equation}
for $\l > \lzero$, where the inclusion is the nesting condition of the input together with the recursion in~\eqref{eq:marked_face_family}. At $\l = \lzero$ it holds because $\M_\lzero$ contains every level-$\lzero$ face.

The union on the left of~\eqref{eq:face_quadrisection_nesting} is not disjoint. A marked face that already lies in $\ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}$ carries its children in $\M_{\l+1}$ already, so quadrisecting it refines nothing and enlarges no region. Only the rest of the slice is actually refined and thus it is the set that Section~\ref{subsec:padding} is concerned with. We use the shorthand
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:newly_refined_slice}
    \simplexsubsetNew{\l} \; \coloneq \; \simplexsubsetTwo{\l} \, \setminus \, \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}}
\end{equation}
for the faces of the domain sequence $\domseq[\L]$ that $\quadriF$ refines for the \emph{first} time. The left of~\eqref{eq:face_quadrisection_nesting} then reads
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:ancestor_patch_enlargement}
    \ancestorPatch{\M'_{\l+1}} \; = \; \ancestorPatch{\M_{\l+1}} \, \sqcup \, \simplexsubsetNew{\l} ,
\end{equation}
so the operator enlarges the ancestor patch on level $\l$ by exactly $\simplexsubsetNew{\l}$ and by nothing else. A slice with $\simplexsubsetNew{\l} = \varnothing$ leaves level $\l$ untouched however large it is.

The levels can also be walked one at a time, which is how an implementation will evaluate the operator. Refining the slices in increasing order gives the same result as one application, because each level adjoins a set that depends on the input slice alone and not on the region it is adjoined to. Only the hypothesis cares about the order. A slice above level $\lzero$ lies inside its region once the slice below it has been refined and not before, unless every slice already lies in $\M_\l$ on the input.

\smallskip

The refinement and padding steps of the adaptive loop in Section~\ref{subsec:adaptive_loop} iterate over the vertex classes $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ of the domain sequence $\domseq[\L]$ and their representatives on level $\l$ as defined in Definition~\ref{def:vertex_class}. The following refinement operator raises a whole neighbourhood of a vertex to a prescribed target level. It adds exactly the class star $\FVanc{\l}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone})$ of the representative vertex $\vertex{\l}{\coarseIdxone}$ of level $\l$ level up to that target, which is what makes it close a pinch, cf.\ Corollary~\ref{cor:pinch_spent}. Whether a class star has to be added on a given level is decided by the leaf star, which is what Proposition~\ref{proposition:class_star_levels} settles.

Since the class star on level $\l$ depends only on the vertex class and the uniform meshes and not on the domain sequence, several vertices can be raised at once by taking the union, and no order has to be fixed. This is the form in which Section~\ref{subsec:adaptive_loop} uses the operator. Each of its steps supplies a set of vertices and a target level for each of them.

\begin{figure}
\begin{center}

\caption{Two leaf meshes before and after applying the graded quadrisection operator from Definition~\robert{\ref{def:graded_vertex_quadrisection}} to a vertex (yellow) on the interface between level $0$ and level $2$.
