    % panels, so it shows what the figure actually occupies.
    \ifnum\candbox=1
        \draw[magenta, dashed, line width=0.4pt]
            (current bounding box.south west) rectangle
            (current bounding box.north east);
    \fi
    \end{tikzpicture}

    \caption{One iteration of successive pinch resolution and confinement padding. Both substeps run two cycles stacked top to bottom. Each of these cycle panels carries two sets. In red, the candidate set of its own substep, i.e. the vertices of~\eqref{eq:pinch_candidates} in the pinch column and the faces of~\eqref{eq:confined_candidates} in the confinement column. In blue, what that substep accumulates for the other one, the faces of~\eqref{eq:pinch_handover} in the pinch column and the vertices of~\eqref{eq:confined_handover_1} in the confinement column. Both blue sets are unions over the productive cycles, so they grow with every cycle. Vertices are drawn as dots and faces as a smaller copy of the face inside itself, and both are coloured by the level they sit on according to the two legends. Both substeps need their second cycle. Cycle $1$ of the pinch column repairs the pinch of the input and opens a new one next to it, and cycle $1$ of the confinement column leaves children that are confined in turn, which is Lemma~\ref{lemma:confined_children}. A third cycle of either detects no new defects (not marked in the panels). The arrow into the output carries $\V^{\mathrm{seed},2}$ of~\eqref{eq:pad_step_pinch}, the vertices this pass hands to the pinch resolution of the next outer loop introduced in Section~\ref{subsec:padding_loop}. That set is the blue one of the bottom confinement panel, and no vertex of it is a pinch of the output. Thus, the second pass is unproductive and the padding loop of Section~\ref{subsec:outer_loop} stops with $q = 1$ here.}
    \label{fig:candidate_sets}
\end{figure}

\FloatBarrier

\subsubsection{Grading leaf stars}
\label{subsubsec:grading}

% 2026-09-07: cut, the third statement of this after main.tex Section~\ref{subsec:padding}
% opening and the paragraph introducing the two admissibility substeps.
% \tobecut{The grading substep is the last of the three padding substeps.}
Grading closes the level span of Definition~\ref{def:level_range} at the vertex classes of $\domseq^{\mathrm{confined}}$ by raising $\lminIdx{\coarseIdxone}$. Unlike the pinch resolution and the confinement padding, grading is optional for admissibility. No clause of Definition~\ref{def:subdiv_partition} mentions the level span, and the other two substeps discharge all three clauses without it. We choose to run grading because it helps the transport of estimator mass across the levels because it targets the coarse end of the leaf star of any vertex class. The marking of Section~\ref{subsubsec:refine_vertex} ranks vertex classes by the area density~\eqref{eq:vertex_residual}, so a coarse face away from the refined region ranks last and is never selected. Nothing else in the loop raises such a face either. Estimator mass sitting on it therefore stays there, while the bulk criterion~\eqref{eq:doerfler_selection} goes on counting it. The grading is the one step that moves that mass up a level and allows the estimator to be recognised for marking.

\smallskip

This substep detects large level spans with Definition~\ref{def:level_range} and repairs them with the graded vertex quadrisection $\quadriV$. The predicate is the maximum level span $g$ of~\eqref{eq:grade_set}. We use $g = 2$ throughout, which works well in the simulations of Section~\ref{subsec:vector_laplace}.

The detection of the grading step selects a set of classes and not of vertices because the level span is a property of the class. It returns the pair of vertex classes and target levels that $\quadriV$ asks for, similar as for the pinch resolution.

\begin{definition}[Grading detection]
    \label{def:grading_detection}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ and let $\vertexClasses^{\mathrm{cand}} \subseteq \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ be a set of candidate classes. \emph{Grading detection} is the map
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_detection}
        \detectG \big( \domseq[\L], \, \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{cand}} \big) \; \coloneq \; \big( \gradeClasses, \, \targetlevel \big)
    \end{equation}
    onto the classes caught among the candidates,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_detection_classes}
        \gradeClasses \; \coloneq \; \big\{ \vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{cand}} \; \colon \; \levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} > g \; \text{ in } \; \domseq[\L] \big\},
    \end{equation}
    and their target level map
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_detection_target}
        \targetlevel_\coarseIdxone \; \coloneq \; \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} - g .
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}

Run on all of $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ the detection catches every class of level span above $g$, which is exactly $\gradeClasses(\domseq[\L])$ of~\eqref{eq:grade_set}. It is the predicate set of the first grading substep, and it is what the exhaustiveness statements of Section~\ref{subsec:outer_loop} and Section~\ref{subsec:termination} compare a seed against.

Applying the grading substep once does not leave a domain sequence of level span at most $g$, and this is where the grading differs from the other two substeps. The reason for that is that grading one class can widen the leaf star of another. Corollary~\ref{cor:graded_quadrisection_leafstar} bounds that widening by one level and the paragraph after it shows the bound is attained, so the grading sweep closes the span to $g \! + \! 1$. That is Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span} below. The residual is left to the next padding cycle, and that is why the grading needs no inner iteration of its own. The grading predicate is one of the three tested on every cycle, so a class sitting at $g \! + \! 1$ is caught again and repaired like any other defect, and by Section~\ref{subsec:termination} a fixed point of $\textsc{Pad}$ carries level span at most $g$.

The sweep collects candidates for all three substeps of the next cycle and consumes none of them itself. It is a graded vertex quadrisection, hence a face quadrisection on the family~\eqref{eq:graded_marked_family} of class stars by Corollary~\ref{cor:graded_is_face_quadrisection}, so the two maps of Definition~\ref{def:candidate_collection} apply. The third map of that definition is stated for the same arbitrary marked family, so all three collections are read on the same two arguments.

\begin{sweep}[Grading]
\label{sw:grading_sweep}
    Let $\vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}} \subseteq \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}})$ be a \emph{seed} of vertex classes on the domain sequence $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}$ produced by the confinement padding substep. The grading step runs the following three steps once.
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=3pt, itemsep=-2pt]
    \item Detect the classes of large level span among the candidates,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_sweep_detection}
        \big( \gradeClasses, \, \targetlevel \big) \; \coloneq \; \detectG \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}, \, \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}} \big).
    \end{equation}
    \vspace{-7mm}
    \item Repair the detected classes by homogenising every caught class to its target,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_sweep_repair}
        \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{grading}} \; \coloneq \; \quadriV \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}, \, \gradeClasses, \, \targetlevel \big).
    \end{equation}
    \item Collect the candidates of the next padding cycle. Its pinch resolution and its confinement padding are seeded from the marked family $\simplexsubsetTwo{}$ that~\eqref{eq:graded_marked_family} builds from $\gradeClasses$ and $\targetlevel$, and its grading from the classes of Definition~\ref{def:candidate_collection} read on the same family,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_sweep_candidates}
        \V^{\mathrm{cand}} \coloneq \collectV \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}, \, \simplexsubsetTwo{} \big)
        \qquad \text{and} \qquad
        \F^{\mathrm{cand}} \coloneq \collectF \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}, \, \simplexsubsetTwo{} \big),
    \end{equation}
    \vspace{-6mm}
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_sweep_classes}
        \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{cand}} \; \coloneq \; \collectC \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}, \, \simplexsubsetTwo{} \big) .
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{sweep}

The sweep admits a sharper reading of its own candidates than Corollary~\ref{cor:span_candidates} gives. That corollary bounds the defects a repair \emph{introduces}. On a sweep whose seed already covers the defects of its input, the candidates cover the defects the sweep \emph{leaves behind} as well, and the set difference can be dropped.

\begin{corollary}[Grading candidates cover the residual]
    \label{cor:grading_candidates_cover}
    Let $\vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}} \subseteq \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ cover the defects of $\domseq[\L]$ so that $\gradeClasses(\domseq[\L]) \subseteq \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}}$. The detection $\detectG(\domseq[\L], \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}})$ then catches exactly $\gradeClasses(\domseq[\L])$, and we write $\targetlevel$ for its target level map. Let $\domseq[\L]' \coloneq \quadriV(\domseq[\L], \gradeClasses(\domseq[\L]), \targetlevel)$ be the sweep on that seed, with marked family $\simplexsubsetTwo{}$ given by~\eqref{eq:graded_marked_family}. A defect of $\domseq[\L]$ that the sweep leaves of level span above $g$ is a changed class and violates~\eqref{eq:span_stays}, so
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:grading_candidates_cover}
        \gradeClasses \big( \domseq[\L]' \big) \; \cap \; \gradeClasses \big( \domseq[\L] \big) \; \subseteq \; \collectC{}^{,c} \big( \domseq[\L], \, \simplexsubsetTwo{} \big) .
    \end{equation}
    Together with Corollary~\ref{cor:span_candidates} this gives $\gradeClasses ( \domseq[\L]' ) \subseteq \collectC ( \domseq[\L], \simplexsubsetTwo{} )$, so the candidates cover every defect of the output and not only the ones the sweep introduced.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \gradeClasses(\domseq[\L]')$. If the sweep created the class it lies in $\collectC{}^{,n}$, and if $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} \leq g$ then Corollary~\ref{cor:span_candidates} applies, so assume $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L])$ with $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} > g$. The hypothesis on the seed puts the class into $\vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}}$, so~\eqref{eq:grading_detection_classes} catches it and~\eqref{eq:grading_detection_target} assigns it the target $\targetlevel_\coarseIdxone = \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} \! - \! g$. The constraint~\eqref{eq:homogenisation_constraint} then raises its coarsest level to $\lminIdx{\coarseIdxone}' \geq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} \! - \! g$, and $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone}' > g$ forces $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}' > \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$. By Corollary~\ref{cor:graded_is_face_quadrisection} the sweep is the face quadrisection on $\simplexsubsetTwo{}$, so clause $(i)$ of Lemma~\ref{lemma:span_opens} gives $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}' = \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ whenever~\eqref{eq:span_stays} holds at the class. Again~\eqref{eq:span_stays} fails there and~\eqref{eq:class_candidate_terms} puts $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ into $\collectC{}^{,c}$.
\end{proof}

Corollary~\ref{cor:grading_candidates_cover} is the one statement of the three that drops the set difference, and it is the one the grading step uses. It supplies the hypothesis that Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span} later places on the seed of the next grading sweep in the overall padding loop.

The seed $\vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}}$ may always be taken to be all classes $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}})$ of the input. A smaller seed can be used whenever that sequence is already known to carry no class of level span above $g$ outside a given set, which is the case in every pass of the padding loop after the first, by Corollaries~\ref{cor:span_candidates} and~\ref{cor:grading_candidates_cover} read on the three repairs of the pass before it. The seed is an argument so that Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span} can state the hypothesis it uses.

\begin{proposition}[What one grading sweep achieves]
    \label{prop:grading_span}
    Let the seed cover the classes of large level span in the input so that $\gradeClasses(\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}) \subseteq \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed}}$. Then every class of $\vertexClasses(\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}})$ has level span at most $g \! + \! 1$ on the output $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{grading}}$ of the grading sweep~\ref{sw:grading_sweep}. If in addition the detection~\eqref{eq:grading_sweep_detection} catches nothing, then $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{grading}} = \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}}$ and every class has level span at most $g$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \vertexClasses(\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined}})$. If it is caught, the constraint~\eqref{eq:homogenisation_constraint} of Lemma~\ref{lemma:graded_quadrisection_minimal} raises the coarsest level to $\lminIdx{\coarseIdxone}' \geq \targetlevel_\coarseIdxone = \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} \! - \! g$, and $\targetlevel_\coarseIdxone \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$ makes~\eqref{eq:graded_quadrisection_leafstar_set} read $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}' \leq \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} \! + \! 1$ at the finest level. Subtracting gives $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone}' \leq g \! + \! 1$, irrespective of the level span before refinement. If it is not caught then $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} \leq g$ in the input because the detection~\eqref{eq:grading_detection_classes} is exhaustive by the hypothesis on the seed. The class is unmarked, so~\eqref{eq:graded_quadrisection_leafstar_unmarked} applies in place of the two bounds above. Its coarsest level cannot fall and its finest level rises by at most one, so $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone}' \leq \levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} \! + \! 1 \leq g \! + \! 1$. Finally, an empty detection makes the marked family~\eqref{eq:graded_marked_family} empty, so~\eqref{eq:grading_sweep_repair} adjoins nothing and the output is the input, on which the hypothesis on the seed and~\eqref{eq:grading_detection_classes} together leave no class of level span above $g$.
\end{proof}

The proposition speaks only about the classes the input carries and not about the ones the sweep creates. Thus, newborn vertices at edge midpoints are not included in the bound and the candidate collection in~\eqref{eq:grading_sweep_classes} has to carry them explicitly. The last sentence of the proposition statement is what Section~\ref{subsec:termination} uses to certify that the $g \! + \! 1$ never survives the loop.

\robert{Table~\ref{tab:grading_ab} of Section~\ref{subsubsec:cl_performance} runs the $\L = 4$ adaptive loop with and without the grading, and reports what the substep costs and what it returns.}

\FloatBarrier

\subsection{The padding loop}
\label{subsec:padding_loop}

\robert{The three substeps of Section~\ref{subsec:padding} each discharge their own predicate and each can reopen another. What closes them into a step of the adaptive loop is an iteration around all three. This section defines that iteration and the candidates it moves between the substeps, then shows that it stops and that it stops at an admissible domain sequence, and closes with what is known and not known about the number of passes it needs.}

\robert{One reading of Definition~\ref{def:candidate_collection} sets the two inner iterations of Section~\ref{subsec:padding} apart, and it is what the loop below has to work around. The two terms of~\eqref{eq:candidate_collection_faces} are the two directions in which the padding frontier can advance, and they are the changed states and the new objects of Section~\ref{subsubsec:locality_candidates} seen geometrically. The one-ring term $\collectF{}^{,c}$ moves it sideways, since it hands on leaf faces that sit at the level the repair was read on. The children term $\collectF{}^{,n}$ moves it one level down, since it hands on faces that are born a level finer. Which of the two a substep keeps is what decides the shape of its bound.

The confinement padding keeps only the children. Lemma~\ref{lemma:confined_children} drops the one-ring term, so the frontier cannot move sideways, the coarsest defect level rises by at least one per cycle, and Corollary~\ref{cor:confinement_cycles} is a level count bounded by $\L$. The pinch resolution keeps both terms. Its frontier can travel sideways at a fixed level, which is the chain of Remark~\ref{rem:padding_chain} without the survival condition that remark adds, and no level count can bound it. That is the difference between the two inner iterations, and it is also why Problem~\ref{prob:padding_passes} is posed for the outer count and not for either inner one.}

\subsubsection{The outer loop}
\label{subsec:outer_loop}

The three substeps of Section~\ref{subsec:padding} compose into the padding cycle
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pad_operator}
    \textsc{Pad} \coloneq \textsc{Grade} \circ \textsc{PadConfinedFaces} \circ \textsc{ResolvePinches}
    \; \colon \; \alldomseqs \; \longrightarrow \; \alldomseqs,
\end{equation}
whose first two factors are the iterations of Section~\ref{subsubsec:pinch_resolution} and Section~\ref{subsubsec:confined_faces} and not single sweeps. Our implementation wraps $\textsc{Pad}$ in a loop that stops once a pass no longer detects any defects, and a counter records the number $q$ of \emph{productive} passes. Written out, one pass runs the three substeps in the order~\eqref{eq:pad_operator} fixes and moves three types of candidate between them. Each substep is one step of the loop, and the inner cycles of Section~\ref{subsubsec:pinch_resolution} and Section~\ref{subsubsec:confined_faces} run inside their own step.

\begin{iteration}[Padding]
\label{it:pad_iteration}
    Write $\domseq[\L]$ for the domain sequence the \textsc{Refine} step of Section~\ref{subsec:refine} refines, and $\simplexsubsetTwo{\mathrm{refine}}$ for the marked family it refined, so that $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}} = \textsc{Refine} ( \domseq[\L] )$. The padding loop is initialised with the domain sequence $\domseq[\L]^{0} \coloneq \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}}$ and with the candidates that step leaves behind,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:pad_initial_seeds}
        \begin{aligned}
            \V^{\mathrm{seed},1} \; &\coloneq \; \collectV \big( \domseq[\L], \, \simplexsubsetTwo{\mathrm{refine}} \big) ,
            \qquad
            \F^{\mathrm{grading},0} \; \coloneq \; \collectF \big( \domseq[\L], \, \simplexsubsetTwo{\mathrm{refine}} \big) , \\[2pt]
            \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{grading},0} \; &\coloneq \; \collectC \big( \domseq[\L], \, \simplexsubsetTwo{\mathrm{refine}} \big) ,
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    All three collections are read on $\domseq[\L]$, the sequence the repair was applied to, and on the family it refined, as Definition~\ref{def:candidate_collection} asks. \robert{Section~\ref{subsec:refine} offers two atoms and the class line has to serve both. It does, because clause $(i)$ of Lemma~\ref{lemma:span_opens} holds for an arbitrary marked family, so~\eqref{eq:pad_initial_seeds} covers the face variant of Section~\ref{subsubsec:refine_face} as well, whose marked faces are leaf faces and need not be confined.} The padding loop then iterates the following \emph{padding pass} for $p = 1, \, 2, \, \ldots \;$:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=3pt, itemsep=-2pt]
    \item Resolve the pinches of the pass input with Iteration~\ref{it:pinch_iteration}, seeded by the vertices the previous pass handed over, and keep the faces and classes its cycles accumulated in~\eqref{eq:pinch_handover},
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:pad_step_pinch}
        \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pinch},p}, \; \F^{\mathrm{pinch},p}, \; \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{pinch},p} \big) \; \coloneq \; \textsc{ResolvePinches} \big( \domseq[\L]^{p-1}, \, \V^{\mathrm{seed},p} \big) ,
    \end{equation}
    where $\V^{\mathrm{seed}, p} = \V^{\mathrm{confined},p-1} \, \cup \, \V^{\mathrm{grading},p-1}$ are the seed vertices of the pinch resolution substep.
    \item Pad the confined faces of that output with Iteration~\ref{it:confined_iteration}, seeded by the faces of step $1$ together with those the grading of the previous pass handed over, and keep the vertices and classes its cycles accumulated in~\eqref{eq:confined_handover_1} and~\eqref{eq:confined_handover_2},
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:pad_step_confined}
        \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined},p}, \; \V^{\mathrm{confined},p}, \; \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{confined},p} \big) \; \coloneq \; \textsc{PadConfinedFaces} \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pinch},p}, \, \F^{\mathrm{seed}, p} \big),
    \end{equation}
    where $\F^{\mathrm{seed}, p} =  \F^{\mathrm{pinch},p} \, \cup \, \F^{\mathrm{grading},p-1}$ are the seed faces of the confinement padding substep.
    \item Grade that output with Sweep~\ref{sw:grading_sweep}, seeded by the classes of steps $1$ and $2$ together with those the grading of the previous pass handed over,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:pad_step_grading_seed}
        \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed},p} \; \coloneq \; \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{pinch},p} \, \cup \, \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{confined},p} \, \cup \, \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{grading},p-1} ,
    \end{equation}
    \vspace{-7mm}
    \item[] and keep the three sets it hands forward,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:pad_step_grading}
        \big( \domseq[\L]^{p}, \; \V^{\mathrm{grading},p}, \; \F^{\mathrm{grading},p}, \; \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{grading},p} \big) \; \coloneq \; \textsc{Grade} \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined},p}, \, \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed},p} \big) .
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\robert{The grading seed~\eqref{eq:pad_step_grading_seed} is the only one of the three with three terms. Neither of the other two substeps marks a class for its level span, so a class already above $g$ has to be carried over from the repair before it.} We repeat steps $1$ to $3$ until no substep detects anything, that is until the pinch detection~\eqref{eq:pinch_round_detection} of step~$1$, the confinement detection~\eqref{eq:confined_cycle_detection} of step~$2$ and the grading detection~\eqref{eq:grading_sweep_detection} of step~$3$ all catch nothing the first time they are evaluated. The same condition read off what the pass collected is
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pad_stopping}
    \F^{\mathrm{pinch},p} \; = \; \varnothing, \qquad \V^{\mathrm{confined},p} \; = \; \varnothing, \qquad \V^{\mathrm{grading},p} \; = \; \varnothing ,
\end{equation}
one set from each substep, since each of the three is collected from the faces its own substep refined. We then set $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pad}} \coloneq \domseq[\L]^{p}$ and write $q \coloneq p \! - \! 1$ for the number of productive passes.
\end{iteration}

%  Signal-flow diagram of one padding pass.
%  Convention: a signal ENTERS a substep on its left face and
%  LEAVES on its right face, so every feedback path runs out to
%  the right, around, and back in on the left. The four port
%  heights are the same at all three substeps.
%  The two admissibility channels run BELOW, the class channel
%  alone runs ABOVE, so the one channel with three sources is
%  isolated and crossing-free.

%  solid box inside it is one cycle and carries the self-loop.

    \draw[arr] (3.58, 0) -- node[sig, above] {$\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pinch},p}$\\\eqref{eq:pad_step_pinch}} (9.42, 0);
    \draw[arr] (16.58, 0) -- node[sig, above] {$\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined},p}$\\\eqref{eq:pad_step_confined}} (21.42, 0);
    \draw[arr] (28.58, 0) -- (33.3, 0);
    \node[sig] at (30.4, 1.35) {$\domseq[\L]^{p}$\\\eqref{eq:pad_step_grading}};

    % ---- port scale: a port's height is the type of candidate it carries
    \node[plab] at (35.6, 4.3) {port height};
    \draw[pscale] (34.0, 3.0) -- (34.0, -3.8);
    \draw[pscale] (34.0, 3.0) -- (34.9, 3.0);

    \caption{One pass of the padding loop of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration}, drawn as a signal flow diagram. Every input enters a substep on its left side and every output leaves on its right side. The dashed boxes are the substeps of the outer loop in the dotted box. The input and output slots of all substeps are grouped by type as indicated by the scale on the right (grey). The solid box inside each substep is one cycle, and the self-loop that touches it is the inner iteration of Section~\ref{subsubsec:pinch_resolution} or Section~\ref{subsubsec:confined_faces}, with its own candidate set and its own cycle count. The grading carries no self-loop because it is a single sweep. The two channels that restore admissibility run below and the class channel runs above on its own, since it is the only one with three sources. The channels that have no predecessor in pass~$1$ are read from the initial seeds of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration}. Every label names a set from the perspective of the substep producing it at pass $p$. A path that runs right to left crosses into the next pass, which the marker $p\!+\!1$ on that path records. The confinement padding drawn here therefore receives $\F^{\mathrm{grading},p-1}$ along the edge labelled $\F^{\mathrm{grading},p}$, and the vertex channel makes the same shift explicit in $\V^{\mathrm{seed},p+1}$.}
    \label{fig:padding_signal_flow}
\end{figure}

The iteration is $q$ applications of the operator~\eqref{eq:pad_operator}, so we write the padding step as
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pad_power}
    \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pad}} \; = \; \textsc{Pad}^{q} \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}} \big),
    \qquad \textsc{Pad}^{q} \; \colon \; \alldomseqs \; \longrightarrow \; \alldomseqs .
\end{equation}
The seeds do not appear there, and they are not an independent variable. The first pass may always detect against the global sets $\V^{\mathrm{pinch}}$, $\F^{\mathrm{confined}}$ and $\gradeClasses$ of $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}}$ from Section~\ref{subsec:padding}, and every later seed is produced inside the iteration by the pass before it. A seed only has to cover the defects of its input, and when it does the detection of each substep is exhaustive, which~\eqref{eq:confined_detection_exhaustive} records for the confinement padding, the invariant in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:pinch_discharged} gives for the pinch resolution, and the hypothesis of Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span} gives for the grading. \robert{That the seeds of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} do cover is Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_seeds_cover} of the next section, which is where~\eqref{eq:pad_detection_exhaustive} states the consequence.} So a seeded substep marks exactly what a full scan would mark, and~\eqref{eq:pad_power} is the map the loop computes however it is seeded.

\robert{The implementation does not seed this way. All three substeps restart from the full scan, which is what Section~\ref{subsec:padding} describes and what the reported counts come from. The saving is real but not on the critical path, since the scan is linear in the mesh and the solve dominates. Measured on the valence-$10$ domain sequence of Figure~\ref{fig:candidate_sets}, the seed that Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} hands the first pinch resolution carries $16$ vertices against the $629$ that the full initial set carries.}

Nothing so far says that the stopping rule of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} ever fires. The question is whether some finite $q$ makes $\textsc{Pad}^{q}$ a map $\alldomseqs \to \allpartitions$, so that finitely many passes restore admissibility whatever the \textsc{Refine} step hands it. Section~\ref{subsec:termination} settles that, and Section~\ref{subsubsec:pass_count} takes up how large $q$ has to be.

\smallskip

The remainder of this section illustrates the padding pass and the candidate sets that couple its substeps. Figure~\ref{fig:padding_signal_flow} draws an individual padding pass as a signal flow, which is Figure~\ref{fig:padding_interactions_b} with the grading step and all the candidates drawn in. The domain sequence runs along the main path and the candidate sets are the side channels, one per type. Each channel ends at the substep that consumes it, and carries what the substeps ahead of it produced in the same pass together with what the substeps behind it produced in the pass before. Which of the two a set is can be read off the figure, since a set spent inside the pass runs forward and a set carried over to the next pass runs back. As a result of the setup of the outer padding loop, no substep ever scans the whole domain sequence, and each one starts from what the substeps before it refined. Remark~\ref{rem:padding_order} discusses the order of substeps.

Three substeps and three candidate types give nine interactions which are all drawn in the figure, seven as arrows and two as the self-loops. The figure provides a visual check that none is missing. That the drawn arrows capture all interactions and that no interaction between the substeps travels outside of them, is what the two locality statements give. Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_locality} for the three refinement fan predicates and Lemma~\ref{lemma:leafstar_locality} for the level span both say a predicate can move only where the repair refined, \robert{and Lemma~\ref{lemma:new_objects} covers the objects a repair adds,} so every interaction runs through a candidate set collected from refined faces and none can take another route. \robert{Two of the nine are narrowed below what Definition~\ref{def:candidate_collection} allows, and the confinement padding owns both. The faces it hands to itself are cut to the children it creates by Lemma~\ref{lemma:confined_children}, and the classes it hands on are cut to the ones it creates by clause~$(ii)$ of Lemma~\ref{lemma:span_opens} and Corollary~\ref{cor:confined_class_candidates}. Both drop a changed state term and keep a new object one. The three interactions that hand on vertices need no narrowing of this kind, since~\eqref{eq:candidate_collection} carries no new object term to begin with, by clause~$(i)$ of Lemma~\ref{lemma:new_objects}.}

One rule runs through the whole figure. Each substep iterates on its own candidate type and hands the other two off to other substeps. The pinch resolution circulates vertices in its inner loop and hands on faces and classes, the confinement padding circulates faces and hands on vertices and classes, and the grading circulates classes. The grading circulates them through the outer loop rather than an inner one, so its missing inner loop and the self-return of the class channel are the same fact read twice.

\subsubsection{Termination and admissibility}
\label{subsec:termination}

The first two padding substeps of pinch resolution and confinement padding discharge their own predicates by Proposition~\ref{prop:pinch_discharged} and Proposition~\ref{prop:confined_discharged}, and the grading leaves its predicate one level short by Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span}. However, no single cycle of a substep can discharge its own effect on a different substep, so the cycles have to be iterated. We can think of that as closing the non-self-loops in Figures~\ref{fig:padding_interactions_b} and~\ref{fig:padding_signal_flow}.

This section shows that the outer padding cycle indeed terminates and that its fixed point is an admissible domain sequence.

\begin{lemma}[A caught defect refines at least one face]
    \label{lemma:pad_productive}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \alldomseqs_\L$ and let one of the three detections~\eqref{eq:pinch_round_detection},~\eqref{eq:confined_cycle_detection} and~\eqref{eq:grading_sweep_detection} catch a defect on it. Then the substep repairing it refines at least one face of $\domseq[\L]$, and it refines no face beyond level $\L$. A productive pass of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} therefore refines at least one face.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The pinch resolution and the grading both repair with $\quadriV$. By Corollary~\ref{cor:graded_quadrisection_composition} that operator is the identity only if every marked class has $\lminIdx{\coarseIdxone} \geq \targetlevel_\coarseIdxone$. A caught class has $\lminIdx{\coarseIdxone} < \targetlevel_\coarseIdxone$, by the proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:pinch_rounds} and by the sentence after~\eqref{eq:grading_detection_target}. The confinement padding repairs with $\quadriF$, and the sentence after~\eqref{eq:confined_faces_resolved_subdiv_partition} settles it. No target exceeds $\L$ and~\eqref{eq:marked_face_family} carries no face on level $\L$, so nothing is refined past the finest level.
\end{proof}

The lemma above is what makes the stopping rule of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} sensible. A pass whose three detections catch nothing is the identity, because an empty detection leaves an empty marked family. Conversely a pass that refines no face can have fired no predicate, by the lemma. So the rule stops on exactly the passes that refine nothing, and~\eqref{eq:pad_stopping} reads that off the candidates rather than the detections. The set $\F^{\mathrm{pinch},p}$ is needed there. The sets that cross the pass boundary do not suffice on their own, because the pinch resolution hands its faces to the confinement padding inside the same pass and they never reach the boundary.

Termination of the padding loop follows follows from Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_productive} and the fact that the mesh hierarchy is finite.

\begin{corollary}[The padding loop terminates]
    \label{cor:pad_passes}
    The number $q$ of productive passes of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} is bounded by the room for refinement its input $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}}$ with regions $\M^{\mathrm{refine}}_\l$ leaves inside the hierarchy, i.e.
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:pad_passes}
        q \; \leq \; \sum\limits_{\l = 1}^{\L} \; \big\vert \, \F_\l(\T_\l) \setminus \M^{\mathrm{refine}}_\l \, \big\vert \, .
    \end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Every productive pass refines at least one face by Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_productive}, so some region $\M_\l$ grows. Every $\M_\l$ is bounded by $\F_\l(\T_\l)$ individually. The sum starts at $\l = 1$ since $\F_\lzero(\T_{\lzero}) = \M_\lzero^{\mathrm{refine}}$.
\end{proof}

This is the room the input leaves in the hierarchy, which is what Corollary~\ref{cor:pinch_rounds} gives the inner pinch iteration and by the same argument. However, the chaining mechanism is more constrained for the outer loop, see Section~\ref{subsec:termination}.

Corollary~\ref{cor:pad_passes} shows that the padding loop terminates and thus that its fixed point, the output domain sequence $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pad}}$, exists. The other important property is that $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pad}}$ is admissible. The loop terminates once it no longer detects any defects between the candidate sets. Thus, the crucial question is whether these candidates are exhaustive, i.e. that they would cover every potential defect without ever letting one slip through. The two inner iterations and the grading sweep each settle this on their own input, in Proposition~\ref{prop:pinch_discharged}, Proposition~\ref{prop:confined_discharged} and Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span}, and each does so under a hypothesis on the seed it is handed. What remains is that Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} hands them seeds meeting that condition in every pass.

\begin{lemma}[The seeds of a padding pass cover its defects]
    \label{lemma:pad_seeds_cover}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \allpartitions$ carry level span at most $g$, let $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}} = \textsc{Refine}(\domseq[\L])$ and run Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} on it. Then every pass $p \geq 1$ satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:pad_seeds_cover}
        \V^{\mathrm{pinch}} \big( \domseq[\L]^{p-1} \big) \subseteq \V^{\mathrm{seed},p},
        \quad
        \F^{\mathrm{confined}} \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pinch},p} \big) \subseteq \F^{\mathrm{seed},p},
        \quad
        \gradeClasses \big( \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined},p} \big) \subseteq \vertexClasses^{\mathrm{seed},p} .
    \end{equation}
    Every substep of every pass therefore meets the hypothesis of its own discharge result, and its detection is exhaustive.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    \robert{All three inclusions have one shape. A defect of a substep's input either sat already in the last sequence known to carry none of that kind, or a repair opened it since. The second case is covered because the three collections of Definition~\ref{def:candidate_collection} are supersets of the sites a repair can open, and Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} unions them into the seed of the substep that consumes them. So only the first case needs an induction on $p$.

    \emph{Pass $1$.} The input $\domseq[\L]$ is admissible of level span at most $g$, so every defect of $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}}$ was opened by the \textsc{Refine} step, and~\eqref{eq:pad_initial_seeds} reads the three collections on $\domseq[\L]$ with that step's marked family. The class term needs one observation of its own. The \textsc{Refine} step is the one graded vertex quadrisection of the loop whose target can exceed $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone}$, so clause~$(iii)$ of Lemma~\ref{lemma:span_opens} does not apply at a class it marks. At such a class the target is $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} \! + \! 1$ or $\L$, and~\eqref{eq:homogenisation_constraint} raises the coarse end to it while~\eqref{eq:graded_quadrisection_leafstar_set} leaves the fine end no higher, so the class comes out at level span $0$ and is no defect at all.

    \emph{Pass $p$ to pass $p \! + \! 1$.} For each channel, name the last sequence free of that defect and the repairs since. By Proposition~\ref{prop:pinch_discharged} that sequence is $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pinch},p}$, and the confinement padding and the grading of pass $p$ follow it, which are the two terms of $\V^{\mathrm{seed},p+1}$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:confined_discharged} it is $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined},p}$, followed by the grading of pass $p$ and the pinch resolution of pass $p \! + \! 1$, the two terms of $\F^{\mathrm{seed},p+1}$. For the level span it is $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{confined},p}$ again, followed by the grading of pass $p$ and then the first two substeps of pass $p \! + \! 1$, the three terms of~\eqref{eq:pad_step_grading_seed}. The grading term carries both the classes the sweep created and the ones it caught and left above $g$, by Corollary~\ref{cor:grading_candidates_cover} read on a sweep whose seed covers its own defects, which is the induction hypothesis at pass $p$.}
\end{proof}

Two things follow from Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_seeds_cover}. The detections are exhaustive, so every substep of every pass marks exactly what a full scan of its own input would mark, for example
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pad_detection_exhaustive}
    \detectV \big( \domseq[\L]^{p-1}, \, \V^{\mathrm{seed},p} \big) \; = \; \detectV \big( \domseq[\L]^{p-1}, \, \V(\domseq[\L]^{p-1}) \big) ,
\end{equation}
and the same on the other two types of candidates. Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} therefore computes the map~\eqref{eq:pad_power} whatever seeds it is run with, which is the claim Section~\ref{subsec:outer_loop} makes after that equation. Without a covering seed the two sides can differ, which is why the iteration is written with the seeds and not with the predicate sets, as~\eqref{eq:confined_detection_exhaustive} already records one loop down.

\begin{proposition}[The padding loop restores admissibility]
    \label{prop:pad_terminates}
    Let $\domseq[\L] \in \allpartitions$ carry level span at most $g$ and let $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}} = \textsc{Refine}(\domseq[\L])$. Then the output $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pad}} = \textsc{Pad}^{q}(\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}})$ of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} is admissible of level span at most $g$, so $\textsc{Pad}^q \colon \alldomseqs \to \allpartitions$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The iteration reaches its stopping pass by Corollary~\ref{cor:pad_passes}, so the output exists, and the stopping rule says that the three detections catch nothing there.

    Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_seeds_cover} makes each of them exhaustive, so an empty detection is an empty predicate set and not merely an empty intersection with a seed. Proposition~\ref{prop:pinch_discharged} then gives clauses~$(ii)$ and~$(iii)$ of Definition~\ref{def:subdiv_partition}, Proposition~\ref{prop:confined_discharged} gives clause~$(i)$, and the second clause of Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span} gives level span at most $g$.

    For the characterisation, read $\textsc{Pad}$ with the full seeds of Section~\ref{subsec:padding}. A domain sequence that is admissible of level span at most $g$ catches nothing in any of the three detections, so every substep is the identity on it and thus the fixed point is an admissible domain sequence.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Every domain sequence of the adaptive loop is admissible]
    \label{cor:adaptive_loop_admissible}
    Run the loop of Section~\ref{subsec:adaptive_loop} from the initial domain sequence $\domseq^0 = \F_\lzero(\T_\lzero) \sqcup \bigsqcup_{\l = 1}^{\L} \varnothing$, with every step applying \textsc{Refine} and then \textsc{Pad}. Then every iterate $\domseq^n$ is admissible of level span at most $g$, so the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{theorem:linear_independence} and Theorem~\ref{theorem:exactness} hold at every step of the loop.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    $\domseq^0$ is admissible with level span $0 < g$ and Proposition~\ref{prop:pad_terminates} carries both properties from $\domseq^n$ to $\domseq^{n+1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The loop moves up the order]
    \label{cor:adaptive_loop_chain}
    In the setting of Corollary~\ref{cor:adaptive_loop_admissible}, the domain sequences of the adaptive loop form a chain $\domseq^{0} \subseteq \domseq^{1} \subseteq \dots$ in the order~\eqref{eq:domseq_order}, and the padding contributes strictly whenever it is productive, $\domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{refine}} \subsetneq \domseq[\L]^{\mathrm{pad}}$ for $q \geq 1$. The adaptive spaces nest along the chain by~\eqref{eq:quadrisection_monotone}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Both quadrisection operators only adjoin faces to regions, by~\eqref{eq:def_face_quadrisection_operator} and~\eqref{eq:graded_vertex_quadrisection}, so no step of the loop moves down. A productive pass refines at least one face by Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_productive}, and a refined face contributes children that the region one level finer did not carry.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The order of the padding substeps]
\label{rem:padding_order}
    A single pass never suffices in general, whichever order is used, and the order is not what makes $\textsc{Pad}$ terminate. The proof of Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_productive} uses only that a substep is the identity when its own detection catches nothing, so Proposition~\ref{prop:pad_terminates} holds for every ordering of the three substeps.

    We run pinch resolution first because it refines the most, so that the faces it confines can still be repaired downstream in the same cycle, and the grading last because it has the least to do. That is a preference and not a bound. Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span} closes the level span to $g \! + \! 1$ whatever reaches the grading, so the ordering costs nothing if a leaf star arrives wider than expected.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{The number of passes}
\label{subsubsec:pass_count}


Corollary~\ref{cor:pad_passes} bounds $q$ by the room the input leaves in the hierarchy, which is a bound in the finest mesh and not in the control mesh. What drives $q$ are the links between the substeps and thus the introduced cross-defects from Figure~\ref{fig:padding_interactions_a}. A pinch resolution can confine a face, the confinement padding can create a pinch, and the grading can do either. The grading carries a fourth link, to itself, since it can open a level span at a second class it marked in the same sweep, under the condition~\eqref{eq:span_opens} and no other. Figure~\ref{fig:padding_signal_flow} draws that one as the class channel returning through the outer loop.

The inner counts $q^{\mathrm{pinch}}$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:pinch_rounds} and $q^{\mathrm{confined}}$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:confinement_cycles} are no part of $q$. Each inner iteration runs to a fixed point of its own predicate, so neither substep can hand its own kind of defect to the next pass, by Proposition~\ref{prop:pinch_discharged} and Proposition~\ref{prop:confined_discharged}. This section describes how the repairs applied by successive padding substeps can chain and thus increase $q$. We first bound $q$ in the one regime where the arc arithmetic settles it, then drop its hypotheses one at a time, then bound the one link that admits a bound, and state what is left open.

\smallskip

The count of outer cycles is provable in one regime, and writing the argument out shows how we can track the candidate sets through the cycles. The whole proof is a walk along the channels of Figure~\ref{fig:padding_signal_flow}, reading off at each substep what its output carries and what its properties are.

\begin{lemma}[One productive pass on a regular patch at $\L = 1$]
    \label{lemma:stars_only_bound}
    Let $\T_\lzero$ be closed and let all of its vertices be regular, i.e.
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:regular_patch}
        \partial \T_\lzero \; = \; \varnothing
        \qquad \text{and} \qquad
        \big\vert \FVl{\lzero} \big( \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \big\vert \; = \; 6
        \quad \text{for all} \quad
        \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V_\lzero(\T_\lzero).
    \end{equation}
        Let $\L = 1$, let $\domseq[1] = \F_\lzero(\T_\lzero) \sqcup \varnothing$ be the uniform level-$\lzero$ domain sequence and let $\domseq[1]^{\mathrm{refine}} = \quadriV(\domseq[1], \markedVertexClasses, \targetlevel)$ be the \textsc{Refine} step of Section~\ref{subsubsec:refine_vertex} on any $\markedVertexClasses \subseteq \vertexClasses(\domseq[1])$. Then, $q \leq 1$ and Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} runs at most one productive pass.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Every leaf star of $\domseq[1]$ spans at most $\L = 1$ level, and no repair widens it past that, so $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxone} \leq g$ throughout and the grading detection~\eqref{eq:grading_sweep_detection} returns $\gradeClasses = \varnothing$ on every pass. The grading is therefore absent and a pass of Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} reduces to its first two substeps, with $\V^{\mathrm{grading},p} = \F^{\mathrm{grading},p} = \varnothing$ and hence $\V^{\mathrm{seed},p+1} = \V^{\mathrm{confined},p}$ and $\F^{\mathrm{seed},p} = \F^{\mathrm{pinch},p}$. Also $\M_2 = \varnothing$, so $\refFan{1}(\vertex{1}{\fineIdxone}) = \varnothing$ for all $\vertex{1}{\fineIdxone} \in \T_1$ and every predicate of Section~\ref{subsec:padding} is read on level $\lzero$ alone. Finally every class of $\domseq[1]$ has $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} = \lzero$, so the \textsc{Refine} step of Section~\ref{subsubsec:refine_vertex} assigns $\targetlevel_\coarseIdxone = \min(\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} \! + \! 1, \L) = 1$ to every marked class.

    The following argument tracks the candidate sets for all substeps through Iteration~\ref{it:pad_iteration} and shows that no predicate can fire in the second pass due to properties of the candidates.

    \robert{\emph{Step $1$, what the pinch resolution leaves.} Both the \textsc{Refine} step and every round~\eqref{eq:pinch_round_repair} of Iteration~\ref{it:pinch_iteration} repair with $\quadriV$ at target $1$, which by~\eqref{eq:graded_vertex_quadrisection} adjoins $\Aop{}{\lzero}{1}(\FVanc{\lzero}(\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}))$, the children of a full mesh star. Collecting the classes they homogenise,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:stars_only_R}
        \widehat{\R} \; \coloneq \; \markedVertexClasses \; \cup \bigcup\limits_{t \, \geq \, 1} \pinchClasses[t] \; \subseteq \; \vertexClasses(\domseq[1]) ,
    \end{equation}
    the output $\domseq[1]^{\mathrm{pinch},1}$ of~\eqref{eq:pad_step_pinch} has level-$1$ region
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:stars_only_region}
        \ancestorPatch{\M_1^{\mathrm{pinch},1}} \; = \bigcup\limits_{\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo} \in \R} \FVl{\lzero} \big( \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxtwo} \big) .
    \end{equation}
    Reading~\eqref{eq:stars_only_region} at a vertex $\vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone}$ whose class is not in $\R$ gives its fan as a union of two-face runs,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:stars_only_fan}
        \refFan{\lzero} \big( \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; = \bigcup\limits_{\substack{\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo} \in \R \\ \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxtwo} \, \sim \, \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone}}} \big\{ \, \face{\lzero}{\fineIdxone} \in \FVl{\lzero} \big( \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; \colon \;\; \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxtwo} \in \face{\lzero}{\fineIdxone} \, \big\} ,
    \end{equation}
    read on $\domseq[1]^{\mathrm{pinch},1}$, where $\sim$ is adjacency in $\T_\lzero$ and each term is the pair of faces carrying the edge $\vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxtwo}$, hence a run of two consecutive faces of the mesh star. A vertex whose class does lie in $\R$ has the whole mesh star as its fan, one arc, and Corollary~\ref{cor:pinch_spent} keeps it a single arc for the rest of the loop.

    \emph{Step $2$, what the confinement padding hands on.} Iteration~\ref{it:confined_iteration} marks $\simplexsubsetTwo[t]{} \subseteq \F^{\mathrm{confined}}$ of~\eqref{eq:confined_set} at cycle $t$, and by Definition~\ref{def:confined_face} no face of it carries a refinement-exterior vertex. The set it hands to the next pass is $\V^{\mathrm{confined},1} = \bigcup_r \collectV(\domseq[1]^{\mathrm{confined},r-1}, \simplexsubsetTwo[r]{})$ by~\eqref{eq:confined_handover_1}, so every one of its vertices is a vertex of a face marked at some cycle, and therefore satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:confined_needs_fan}
        \refFan{\lzero} \big( \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \big) \; \neq \; \varnothing
        \qquad \text{on} \qquad \domseq[1]^{\mathrm{confined},r-1}
        \qquad \text{for all} \qquad
        \vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \; \in \; \V^{\mathrm{confined},1} .
    \end{equation}
    No vertex of $\V^{\mathrm{confined},1}$ therefore receives its first fan face from this substep. Each one already carried a fan at the end of step $1$, which by~\eqref{eq:stars_only_fan} contained a run of two consecutive faces, and fans only grow.

    \emph{Step $2$ again, what it removes.} Let $\domseq[1]^{\mathrm{confined},1}$ be the output of~\eqref{eq:pad_step_confined}. Suppose a vertex had a gap of exactly one face $\face{\lzero}{\fineIdxone}$ there, so that both faces flanking $\face{\lzero}{\fineIdxone}$ in its mesh star lie in its fan. Each of the other two vertices of $\face{\lzero}{\fineIdxone}$ lies in one of those two flanking faces, so none of the three vertices of $\face{\lzero}{\fineIdxone}$ is refinement-exterior, and $\face{\lzero}{\fineIdxone}$ is a confined leaf face. That contradicts Proposition~\ref{prop:confined_discharged}. Every gap of $\domseq[1]^{\mathrm{confined},1} = \domseq[1]^{1}$ therefore has length at least two.

    \emph{Step $3$, the detection of the next pass.} The seed is $\V^{\mathrm{seed},2} = \V^{\mathrm{confined},1}$, which is in general not empty, and~\eqref{eq:pinch_round_detection} evaluates $\detectV(\domseq[1]^{1}, \V^{\mathrm{seed},2})$. Let $\vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone} \in \V^{\mathrm{seed},2}$. If $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone} \in \R$ its fan is the whole mesh star, a single arc, and Definition~\ref{def:pinch_vertex} does not fire. Otherwise its fan carries an arc of length at least two by step $2$, and every gap has length at least two by the paragraph above. Two arcs and two gaps would then need eight faces, while $\vert \FVl{\lzero}(\vertex{\lzero}{\coarseIdxone}) \vert = 6$ by~\eqref{eq:regular_patch}, so the fan has one arc and the vertex is no pinch. Boundary pinches do not arise because $\partial \T_\lzero = \varnothing$. Hence $\pinchClasses[1] = \varnothing$ on pass $2$, the repair~\eqref{eq:pinch_round_repair} is the identity and~\eqref{eq:pinch_handover} gives $\F^{\mathrm{pinch},2} = \varnothing$. The confinement padding of pass $2$ then runs on $\F^{\mathrm{seed},2} = \F^{\mathrm{pinch},2} = \varnothing$, so $\simplexsubsetTwo[1]{} = \varnothing$ and~\eqref{eq:confined_handover_1} gives $\V^{\mathrm{confined},2} = \varnothing$. With $\V^{\mathrm{grading},2} = \varnothing$ from the grading being absent, the three conditions of~\eqref{eq:pad_stopping} hold on pass $2$ and the loop stops with $q \leq 1$.}
\end{proof}

\guidance{The hypothesis~\eqref{eq:regular_patch} is global because the reach of the pass is not bounded a priori. Only the vertices the pass actually touches enter the proof, so the argument is local and holds verbatim on a regular interior region whenever the padding stays inside it. What is missing is a statement that it does, which is the first half of Problem~\ref{prob:padding_passes}. Worth saying in prose once that half is settled, or once a reach bound for the pinch resolution exists.}

\robert{Each hypothesis of Lemma~\ref{lemma:stars_only_bound} can be dropped on its own, and each one is a mechanism the paper has already met. The count in the last step of the proof needs eight faces for two arcs and two gaps, and a relaxation either shortens an arc or enlarges the star.}

        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{#1}
        \captionsetup{width=\chaincapwidth}
        \caption{#2}
        \label{#3}
    \end{subfigure}%
}
\begin{figure}[tb]
    \centering
    \chainpanel{images/padding_chain/chain_input.pdf}
        {Input domain sequence $\domseq[\L]$ with a pinch produced by the \textsc{Refine} step.}
        {fig:padding_chain_input}%
    \hfill%
    \chainpanel{images/padding_chain/chain_pass0.pdf}
        {Pass~1 resolves the first pinch, pads one face, and creates a second pinch.}
        {fig:padding_chain_pass1}%
    \hfill%
    \chainpanel{images/padding_chain/chain_pass1.pdf}
        {Pass~2 resolves the second pinch, pads one face, and creates a third pinch.}
        {fig:padding_chain_pass2}%
    \hfill%
    \chainpanel{images/padding_chain/chain_pass2.pdf}
        {Pass~3 resolves the third pinch and pads one face (the last productive pass).}
        {fig:padding_chain_pass3}
    \caption{A control mesh with three interior vertices of valence $8$ in a row on which $\textsc{Pad}$ needs one productive pass per vertex. The three valence-$8$ vertices are drawn in colour, and the one resolved in a pass is emphasised with a ring. \guidance{Check against the generating script that the marking is the four vertex stars of the uniform domain sequence.} Each pass resolves exactly one pinch vertex and pads a single face that has become confined after the pinch resolution. A pinch is resolved by refining the faces of its gaps, so that its arcs merge into one. That padded face creates the next pinch along the vertex chain, so the refinement propagates down one vertex per pass. A fourth, unproductive pass confirms the fixed point.}
    \label{fig:padding_chain}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[\robert{Relaxing the valence}]
    \label{rem:padding_chain}
    \robert{Figure~\ref{fig:padding_chain} runs the chain three times on a row of valence-$8$ vertices, one productive pass per vertex, and its caption follows the passes.}

    \robert{What keeps the chain alive from one link to the next is a gap of two faces. Step $2$ of the proof of Lemma~\ref{lemma:stars_only_bound} shows why a gap of one cannot survive, since it is a confined leaf face and the confinement padding of the same pass refines it. Chains therefore run only where the \textsc{Refine} step leaves gaps of two, which at $\L = 1$ needs a vertex of valence at least eight.}

    \robert{The smallest surviving configuration is two refinement arcs and two gaps of two faces, so its valence is four plus the two arc lengths. At $\L = 1$ a marked neighbour leaves an arc of two faces and the valence has to reach eight, which is the mesh of Figure~\ref{fig:padding_chain}, while a thin arc of one face brings it down to six.}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[\robert{Relaxing the closedness}]
    \label{rem:boundary_chain}
    \robert{At a boundary vertex the fan is linear by Definition~\ref{def:refinement_arcs}, and two steps of the proof fail. The step behind~\eqref{eq:stars_only_fan} reads the faces shared by two mesh stars as the faces carrying the edge between them, which is two for an interior edge and one for a boundary edge, so a marked neighbour across a boundary edge leaves an arc of a single face at the end of the fan. Star marking alone therefore supplies the thin arc that Remark~\ref{rem:regular_chain} needs a second level for. The gap step weakens for the same reason, since a one-face gap at the end of a linear fan has only one flanking face and need not be confined, and a boundary pinch needs one unanchored arc rather than two by Definition~\ref{def:boundary_pinch}. A boundary version of the lemma would need its own threshold, and boundary valence $5$ is already a site.}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[\robert{Relaxing $\L = 1$}]
\label{rem:regular_chain}
Once we allow $\L \geq 2$, even regular vertices can become pinch vertices with two gaps of two coarse faces. Refining the interface of an existing fine patch can lift a single coarse face and create an arc of one face, cf.\ the right-hand pair of Figure~\ref{fig:refine_interface}. The pinch survives the confinement padding step and needs a second pinch resolution. The number of irregular vertices of $\T_\lzero$ therefore does not bound $q$, and we observe a second productive pass on meshes that carry no irregular vertex at all.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[\robert{Relaxing the star marking}]
    \label{rem:face_marking_chain}
    \robert{The face refinement of Section~\ref{subsubsec:refine_face} refines single faces, so it breaks~\eqref{eq:stars_only_fan} at its source and produces an arc of one face with no irregular vertex, no boundary and no second level. It is the cheapest of the four relaxations and the one that makes the staircase configurations of Section~\ref{subsubsec:pass_count} reachable at all.}
\end{remark}

\robert{The two remarks above follow the link the pinch resolution makes. The link the grading makes admits something the pinch link does not, a direction. A grading sweep refines the class star of a caught class $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxtwo}$ only on the levels below $\targetlevel_\coarseIdxtwo = \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxtwo} \! - \! g$ by~\eqref{eq:graded_vertex_quadrisection}, so it can lift the fine end of a second class $\vertexClass{\coarseIdxone}$ only where $\lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxone} < \lmaxIdx{\coarseIdxtwo} \! - \! g$. A grading link therefore runs to a class more than $g$ levels coarser at the fine end.}

\robert{A chain of grading links walks down the hierarchy on that account. The span opens by exactly one level by clause~$(iii)$ of Lemma~\ref{lemma:span_opens}, and of the two substeps running before the next grading the confinement padding adds nothing to the fine end by clause~$(ii)$ of the same lemma and the pinch resolution at most one level by~\eqref{eq:span_bounds}. Each link therefore drops the fine end by at least $g \! - \! 1$, so with the $g = 2$ of Section~\ref{subsubsec:grading} such a chain runs for at most $\L \! - \! \lzero$ passes.}

\robert{This bounds the grading channel and not $q$. A pass stays productive when any one of the three substeps detects something, so a chain running through the pinch link is untouched by the argument, and that is the chain of Remark~\ref{rem:padding_chain} and the one Problem~\ref{prob:padding_passes} below is about. The bound also reads $\L$ and not $\T_\lzero$, so it is not of the shape that problem asks for. What it does settle is that the one level Proposition~\ref{prop:grading_span} leaves behind cannot be handed along for ever.}

\begin{problem}[Bounding the number of padding passes]
    \label{prob:padding_passes}
    Bound the number $q$ of productive $\textsc{Pad}$ passes by a quantity depending only on the control mesh $\T_\lzero$. \checkit{Localise} the faces refined by $\textsc{Pad}$ to a neighbourhood of the defects of $\domseq[\L]$ of bounded size.
\end{problem}

\robert{The two halves are not equally open. For the first, a bound depending only on $\T_\lzero$ need not be a constant, since the staircase configuration rules one out. What such a bound has to survive is the four relaxations above. Remark~\ref{rem:padding_chain} enlarges the star until the eight faces the count needs are available, while Remarks~\ref{rem:boundary_chain},~\ref{rem:regular_chain} and~\ref{rem:face_marking_chain} each shorten an arc to a single face instead, and one domain sequence may carry several of them at once. Lemma~\ref{lemma:class_reach} constrains the walk that results, since a link at level $\l$ needs a class born no earlier than $\l \! - \! 1$ at one of its two ends by~\eqref{eq:meeting_level_bound} and~\eqref{eq:class_reach}. That suggests a bound of the order of the diameter of $\T_\lzero$, which is admissible, and it does not bound the length. We have neither constructed a chain through regular vertices nor excluded one.}

\robert{The second half asks for localisation, and it is there to rule out the degenerate answer $\textsc{Pad}(\domseq[\L]) = \T_\L$, which stabilises in one pass and defeats the purpose of the adaptive algorithm. The padding is local on each level separately by Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_locality}, but a single pinch repair refines on every level from $\lminIdx{\coarseIdxtwo}$ up to the detection level, as does the graded vertex quadrisection of Definition~\ref{def:graded_vertex_quadrisection}, and neighbours on level $\l \! - \! k$ are $2^k$ apart on level $\l$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:distance_doubling}. A neighbourhood of bounded size in the finest mesh is therefore the wrong object to ask for. What one can reasonably hope to bound is the neighbourhood \emph{level by level}, which is what Lemma~\ref{lemma:pad_locality} already delivers, and the number of levels a single repair spans, which is the level span $\levelSpan{\coarseIdxtwo}$ that the grading step exists to control.}

\robert{Where a bound would have to come from is visible by now. Every count in this section and the two before it has one shape. A cycle count is a statement that a candidate set runs out, and every candidate set of Section~\ref{subsec:padding} is collected from the faces a repair refined. The confinement padding runs out because Lemma~\ref{lemma:confined_children} drops the one-ring term, so its frontier can only move down and Corollary~\ref{cor:confinement_cycles} is a level count. The grading channel closes for the same kind of reason, through the targets the grading detection assigns. Neither is a count of defects. Both are constraints on the refinement operator that make its own candidates exhaust themselves. A bound on $q$ is therefore a question about the refinement operators before it is one about the mesh. What is missing is a constraint of that kind for the pinch resolution, one that stops the one-ring term of~\eqref{eq:candidate_collection_faces} from feeding itself indefinitely. That is what Section~\ref{subsec:padding} tracks the refinement front for, and Figure~\ref{fig:padding_signal_flow} is where one reads off which channel is left to close.}

We reiterate how rare padding chains are. The configurations with $q > 1$ above were searched for deliberately, in order to isolate the mechanism of Figure~\ref{fig:padding_chain}, whereas \guidance{every step of the adaptive runs of Section~\ref{subsec:vector_laplace} at $\L = 3$, $4$ and $5$ stabilises after a single productive pass and pads a small fraction of the faces refined by the marking. Re-source before promoting this to prose. The claim came from the column $q$ of Table~\ref{tab:padding_interface_L4} and that table is stale}.


\FloatBarrier


\section{Numerical Experiments}
\label{sec:numerical_experiments}

This section verifies the properties of the adaptive subdivision $k$-form spaces $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ and $\hierSDFspaceZero{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ predicted in Section~\ref{sec:construction_of_hierarchical_subdiv_spaces} and tests the adaptive loop presented in Section~\ref{subsec:adaptive_loop}. Section~\ref{subsec:exactness_atomic} illustrates how domain sequences with pinch vertices or \checkit{confined} faces can break the de~Rham complex and linear independence, and how padding repairs the domain sequences. Section~\ref{subsec:maxwell} then confirms on the Maxwell eigenvalue problem that the spurious modes are \checkit{restricted} to the eigenvalue $0$, \guidance{which reflects that $\extd$ maps $\hierSDFspaceZero{0}{\domseq}{\L}$ into $\hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq}{\L}$ for every domain sequence.} Section~\ref{subsec:vector_laplace} exercises the full adaptive loop on a vector Laplace problem with a singular solution. The emphasis throughout is on the admissibility of the adaptive subdivision $k$-form spaces, i.e. whether the active DoFs are linearly independent and preserve the cohomology of the de Rham complex, rather than on approximation rates.

\subsection{Exactness and padding on atomic partitions}
\label{subsec:exactness_atomic}

Note that panel (e) shows $\dim(\mathring{V}^k)$ and $\dim(\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^k)$ for the spaces $\hierSDFspaceZero{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ with vanishing trace boundary conditions.}

Before applying the adaptive loop to a PDE, we validate the admissibility
construction directly on a suite of domain sequences that represent minimal test cases. This allows us to study the different modes of failure of adaptive subdivision $k$-form spaces and to confirm that padding repairs them correctly so that the assumptions of the Theorems~\ref{theorem:linear_independence} and~\ref{theorem:exactness} are satisfied again. Each domain sequence is a small concentric-ring mesh around a single, irregular vertex of chosen valence at the centre.

We want to verify the linear independence of all active DoFs $\activeDoFSet{k}{}(\domseq)$ of the respective domain sequences $\domseq$. We will test that by computing $\vert \activeDoFSet{k}{}(\domseq) \vert$ by counting elements and $\mathrm{dim}(\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}) \!=\! \mathrm{rank} (\Amat{k}{\domseq}{\L})$. If these two coincide, all active DoFs are indeed linearly independent.

Further, we want to test whether the adaptive $k$-form subdivision spaces $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ possess the correct cohomology. Since the domains are topological disks, we expect the spaces of harmonic $k$-form to have the dimensions $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^0) \!=\! 1$ and $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \!=\! \mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^2) \!=\! 0$ for $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ without boundary conditions. For each domain sequence and its respective adaptive subdivision $k$-form space, we compute $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^k) \!=\! \mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{ker}(\extd^{k})) - \mathrm{rank}(\extd^{k-1})$ explicitly from the rank of the discrete $k$-form derivative $\extd^k$ before and after padding.

\smallskip

Figures~\ref{fig:pinch_resolution} and~\ref{fig:confinement_padding} show the test domain sequences on the left and the padded domain sequences on the right, annotating every domain sequence with its active $k$-form DoF count $\lvert\activeDoFSet{k}{}\rvert$, the true space dimension $\dim V^k = \operatorname{rank}(\Aop{k}{\domseq}{\L})$, and the harmonic dimension $\dim(\mathcal{H}^k)$. Note that we denote $V^k = \hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ here to save horizontal space. The reported data belongs to $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ throughout because the geometric defects of the domain sequence are \checkit{restricted} to the interior and thus, the vanishing-trace spaces $\hierSDFspaceZero{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ exhibit the same algebraic defects and are repaired identically by the padding. The sole exception is the domain sequence in Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_resolution_boundary_pinches}, whose two boundary pinches leave $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ intact and cost a harmonic only under a vanishing trace, so we report $\hierSDFspaceZero{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ there. The unanchored arcs of Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_resolution_unanchored} behave the other way round --- one spurious harmonic $1$-form each in $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$, none under a vanishing trace --- so that panel is reported without boundary conditions like the rest.

\smallskip

We can identify two trends in the data that are consistent with the theoretical predictions. First, Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_resolution} shows that vertex pinches lead to spurious $1$-form harmonics but do not introduce any linear dependencies since $\vert \activeDoFSet{k}{} \vert = \mathrm{dim}(\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L})$ in any case, before and after padding. We can observe that padding indeed removes these spurious $1$-form harmonics.

The individual panels show that the dimensions $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1)$ of the spurious harmonic $1$-form spaces increase with the level difference of the target level and the coarsest level of the faces incident to the pinch vertex. For instance, the domain sequence in Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_level_1} has a pinch on level $1$ with the coarsest level being $0$. This leads to $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \! = \! 1$. In contrast, Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_level_2} has its pinch on level $2$ and $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \! = \! 2$. Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_mixed_level} also has a pinch on level $2$, but the faces between its two refinement arcs mix the levels $0$ and $1$. It is the coarsest level $0$ around the vertex that sets the dimension, not the second finest level $1$: we again observe $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \! = \! 2$, and not $1$.

\guidance{\robert{This rule is empirical and rests on the three cases above.} It is stated
here as an observation, not a consequence of~\cite{companion_paper}, which establishes that a
pinch obstructs the exactness argument but explicitly does not claim the converse --- that
every pinch produces a defect of a predictable dimension. Worth re-checking if further pinch
configurations are added.}

The dimensions $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1)$ of the spurious harmonic $1$-form spaces also increase with the number $\gamma$ of fine arcs around the pinch, see Definition~\ref{def:pinch_vertex}. Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_level_1} has $\gamma = 2$ and $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) = 1$, while Figure~\ref{fig:double_pinch} has $\gamma = 3$ and $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \!=\! 2$.

Figure~\ref{fig:pinch_resolution_boundary_pinches} presents data of the spaces $\hierSDFspaceZero{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ with vanishing boundary conditions, and thus we expect $\mathrm{dim}(\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^0) \!=\! \mathrm{dim}(\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^1) \!=\! 0$ and $\mathrm{dim}(\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^2) \!=\! 1$. The domain sequence in the figure has six pinches at the boundary. The table reports $\mathrm{dim}(\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^1) = 6$ before padding, implying that the spurious harmonics act additively for multiple pinches.

\smallskip

The second observation is concerned with \checkit{confined} faces and the defects they cause. \guidance{The domain sequence in Figure~\ref{fig:coarse_ring} exhibits linear dependence in every form degree: the active $0$-form DoFs have $18$ linear dependencies, the $1$-form DoFs have $36$, and the $2$-form DoFs have $18$. The cohomology, on the other hand, is already correct on the input domain sequence, $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \!=\! 0$, so a closed coarse loop of \checkit{confined} faces costs us linear independence without creating a spurious harmonic.} The padding step refines the entire coarse loop from level $0$ to level $1$.

\guidance{The domain sequence in Figure~\ref{fig:horsehoe} opens the loop, and the picture is the same: linear dependencies but no spurious harmonics. They split into $17$ $0$-form DoFs, $31$ $1$-form DoFs, and $15$ $2$-form DoFs.} The padding space removes these linearly dependent DoFs while maintaining the correct cohomology.

Finally, Figure~\ref{fig:nested_center} shows the prototypical configuration around a vertex that has been refined gradedly by the vertex quadrisection operator from Definition~\ref{def:graded_vertex_quadrisection}. We see that the domain sequence has no \checkit{confined} faces since all vertices become \guidance{refinement-exterior} at just the right level, see Figure~\ref{fig:adm_exterior}. This means that we can run the \textsc{Pad} step unconditionally in the adaptive loop without any risks of over-refining regions that are already \checkit{unconfined}.

\smallskip

Every domain sequence considered so far is a topological disk, so the only harmonic $1$-forms observed have been the defect-driven ones that padding removes. Figure~\ref{fig:genuine_cohomology} shows a domain sequence with a non-trivial topology (two holes). \guidance{The input domain sequence has $15$ linearly dependent $1$-form DoFs and $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \!=\! 4$. After padding, all DoFs are independent again and $\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H}^1) \!=\! 2$ exactly. The padding removed the $2$ spurious harmonics while leaving both genuine harmonics untouched.}

\smallskip

This section confirms that the \textsc{Pad} step correctly restores the geometric defects of the domain sequence produced by the \text{Refine} and makes it a admissible domain sequence. Thus, it also restores the important algebraic properties of linear independence and exactness of the complex of the adaptive subdivision $k$-form spaces, both with and without vanishing trace boundary conditions.

\subsection{Maxwell Eigenvalue problem}
\label{subsec:maxwell}

This section studies the Maxwell eigenvalue problem, a classical test case to verify the properties of discrete differential complexes. Section~\ref{subsec:exactness_atomic} has already established that the \textsc{Pad} step restores the correct cohomology for the spaces $\hierSDFspace{k}{\domseq}{\L}$ by repairing the geometric defects of the domain sequence $\domseq$ so that it becomes a admissible domain sequence. \guidance{In contrast}, this section studies how the spurious harmonic $1$-forms of a domain sequence concretely affect the solution of the eigenproblem.

The weak form of the Maxwell cavity (curl--curl) eigenvalue problem on the domain $\M = [0,\pi]^2$ with mesh $\T_\L$ is given by: Find $\lambda_{h,\coarseIdxone} \in \mathbb{R}$ and $u_\coarseIdxone \in \hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq}{\L}$ such that
\begin{equation}
  (\extd u_i, \extd v)_\M = \lambda_{h,\coarseIdxone} (u_i, v)_\M \qquad \forall v \in \hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq}{\L}
\end{equation}
with analytic spectrum $\lambda_\coarseIdxone = m^2 + n^2$, where $m$ and $n$ are integers. We will approximate the discrete spectra on the domain sequence $\domseq^{\mathrm{pinch}}$ with a pinch on level $\l=1$ as displayed in Figure~\ref{fig:Maxwell_inexact_DoFmesh} and its padded version, the admissible domain sequence $\domseq^{\mathrm{padded}}$ from Figure~\ref{fig:Maxwell_exact_DoFmesh}. Since the domain is contractible, its relative first Betti number is zero. Thus, $\hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq^{\mathrm{padded}}}{\L}$ has no $1$-form harmonics because $\domseq^{\mathrm{padded}}$ is a admissible domain sequence. The pinch of $\domseq^{\mathrm{pinch}}$ introduces a one-dimensional spurious harmonic $1$-form space into $\hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq^{\mathrm{pinch}}}{\L}$.

Applying Loop subdivision alters the domain slightly, so we do not compare the discrete spectrum directly with the analytic spectrum  $\lambda_\coarseIdxone$. Instead, we compare the discrete spectra $\lambda_\coarseIdxone$ with the spectrum of $\FEECspaceZero{k}{\L}$ with $\domseq^{\mathrm{FEEC}} = \T_\L$, a superspace of both $\hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq^{\mathrm{pinch}}}{\L}$ and $\hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq^{\mathrm{padded}}}{\L}$ that verifiably converges to the correct spectrum, see~\cite{Arnold.FEEC}.

Figure~\ref{fig:Maxwell_eigenvalues} reports the discrete spectra with $\lambda_{h,\coarseIdxone} > 0$ for all three domain sequences. Outside of small approximation errors, the spectra agree across all the three
partitions. That means that the defects caused by the spurious $1$-form harmonics of $\hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq^{\mathrm{pinch}}}{\L}$ are \checkit{restricted} to the eigenspace of $\lambda_{h,\coarseIdxone} = 0$. The reason for that behaviour is Theorem~\ref{theorem:differential_complex}, stating that \guidance{$\extd \hierSDFspaceZero{0}{\domseq}{\L} \subseteq \hierSDFspaceZero{1}{\domseq}{\L}$ holds} for all domain sequences $\domseq \in \guidance{\alldomseqs}$, regardless of whether they satisfy the admissible domain sequence conditions. \guidance{Every gradient of an active $0$-form is therefore itself an active $1$-form and is annihilated by $\extd$, so it contributes to the eigenvalue $0$ rather than to the non-zero spectrum, cf.~\cite{Boffi.2010}.}
